Strategy in one line. The most quantitative interviewer on the panel gets the Elementum stories as primaries — ten, each with a real analytical spine (distribution, sample size, interval, counterfactual, sensitivity) and substituted worked numbers. Lead with S8 (measured lanes → p99 promise → ~$1.5M released, zero stockouts) or S6 (honest supply → shortage alert a lead-time early → $2M released under a two-signal rule); go deeper with S8a (the distribution analysis), S8b (the safety-stock formula redesign), S8c (the back-test) and S9 (1,000 lanes → 597); reach for the Walmart Marketplace delivery-experience stories (S19, S20, S21) and the new-market newsvendor design (E7) as the second and third examples. The two lane programs' dollars never meet. In his language: S6 positions safety stock where measured errors justify it; S8 positions the promise at p99 per lane. Every dollar keeps its basis word — record · user anchor · modeled · illustrative.
LP focus — my guess, not confirmed: Dive Deep · Are Right, A Lot · Learn & Be Curious — with a mentorship angle (his public About line names "a culture of mentorship"; expect one people question). Amazon does not disclose the assignment; the guesses are reasoned from the interviewer's title, the JD and his public profile in "Read me first" (public-profile inference, labeled as such) and should be held loosely — Insist on the Highest Standards remains a live alternate via the 99% bar in S8.
Shadow etiquette: address both; the shadow is usually a new interviewer being calibrated — same numbers, same pace; if the shadow asks the last question, answer it with the same discipline (phase asked, then stop).

How to use this packet in 90 seconds. Page A is the cram sheet. Page B routes any question to a story, an angle, a 15-second opener and one number. Every primary has four behavioral questions, three angle cards and three trap drills — the trap drills are the statistical follow-ups this interviewer is most likely to throw. Anything tagged VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH is a detail that is not in the record: confirm it or leave it out; the one-page checklist near the back collects them all.

A · 5-minute cram sheet — Ryan Hoerger, Placement Analytics NEW

Who is in the room (public-profile inference — framing only, never quoted back)
  • Manager, Transfer/Placement Analytics; Chicago Booth MBA (Siebel Scholar); analytics manager at Nike; Cornerstone Research before that — expert-grade statistical work. Shadow: Sandy Hsiao (same numbers, same pace).
  • Expect: definitions and denominators (“how was it measured?”), n and intervals, the counterfactual (“how do you know it was you and not seasonality?”), and “what is your softest input?”
  • Style: claim → number with its basis word → stop. Memo-like, not a wind-up.
Three sentences to open with

“Every dollar I give you carries its basis — record, modeled or illustrative — in the same breath, and I will tell you the n behind every percentage.”

“The planning parameter was wrong before the policy was: the brochure lead time on 200 lanes, and what counted as supply — I measured the first lane by lane and defined the second before any screen existed.”

“I owned the definitions, the policy and the release rule; planners owned the forecast and the buffers, the data team built the distributions, Finance signed each release.”

Five numbers never to get wrong
  • 580K units / ~$1.5M — S8, record, one-time, zero stockouts; $2.59/unit is derived. Never merged with the $2M into a range; never per year.
  • $2M — S6, record, one-time; a different program — never added to the $1.5M.
  • 597 lanes = 80% of spend out of 1,000 asked; completeness 60% → 95% — S9, record. Freight −10% ≈ $2M is six months, not annualized.
  • 99% lead-time accuracy at the p99 promise on 200 lanes — record; know which definition (coverage or ratio) before quoting a tolerance.
  • Six sites — no regional qualifier; S19’s ≈$13.3M/yr and S21’s ≈$3.7M/yr are modeled, never record.
Statistical reflexes (say these without being asked)
  • Give n and the interval: 22 late of 2,500 → 99.1%, Wilson [98.7, 99.4] — illustrative inputs.
  • One held-out quarter is one draw; rolling origin is the upgrade. Zero stockouts bounds the rate: 0 in 1,950 lane-weeks → below 0.154% (rule of three).
  • Name the softest input before he does.
  • NACF bridge: inbound lead-time variability and the definition of supply sit under every WOC target and every placement promise — booklet INV-A (WOC), INV-B (safety stock), INV-C (placement).

Read me first

The interviewer's lens (inference from title, JD and public profile — the profile parts labeled)

Inference from the title. "Placement Analytics" is the analytical arm of inventory placement — deciding where inventory should sit in the network so that the promise holds at the lowest landed cost. A manager in that function lives on forecast error, lead-time variability, safety-stock policy, service levels and the data-quality underneath them. He will be the most quantitative interviewer on the panel and the most likely to ask "how was that measured?", "what was the denominator?", and "what is the softest input in your model?" He is also the one most likely to recognise a textbook formula being passed off as a program artifact — so the honesty labels matter more here than anywhere.

What that means for the answers. Ten Elementum primaries (the router and cram sheet say which to reach for). The first two are the Plan-process redesigns with the math visible: the shortage-alert / real-time supply-demand view (S6 — forecast accuracy 70 → 85%, $2M safety stock released under a two-signal rule) and the measured-lane lead-time program (S8 — promise at p99 on 200 lanes, 580K units / ~$1.5M released with zero stockouts). The other eight are the same facts cut for a statistician — the lead-time distribution (S8a), the safety-stock formula (S8b), the back-test (S8c), the 1,000 → 597 lane prioritization (S9), the Walmart Marketplace delivery promise, WBR and managed-shipping design (S19, S20, S21) and the new-market newsvendor design (E7). The lane programs end on capital released on evidence with service held — asset utilization and reliability in the JD's words. Keep the two programs' dollars apart in every sentence. A shadow (Sandy Hsiao) is on the call: same pace, same numbers, expect one question from the shadow near the end.

What his public profile adds (public-profile inference, labeled)

The profile (public-profile inference — directional, not verified biography). Manager, Transfer/Placement Analytics at Amazon (Jul 2025–), New York; before that Senior Business Intelligence Engineer at Amazon (Dec 2024–Jul 2025); analytics manager at Nike (2021–24); analyst at Cornerstone Research (2016–20) — an economics-consulting shop where the work product is expert-testimony-grade statistical and financial analysis; Chicago Booth MBA (Siebel Scholar); Duke. His own About line: "rigorous statistical and financial analysis… a blend of data analysis and writing skills… a culture of mentorship." Quoted for framing only — never referenced in the room; none of it enters my stories.

What that means for the answers. Expect definition-level probing: MAPE versus bias and which one steered; the grain (SKU-family? per lane?); the window and refresh; what "99% lead-time accuracy" tolerates in days. Three such definition Q&A are drilled into the S6/S8 probing below. Second, answer style: crisp and memo-like — claim, evidence, stop; he writes for a living, and a long wind-up reads as evasion. Third, speak placement: S6 is positioning safety stock where the measured errors justify it; S8 is positioning the promise at p99 per lane — his daily work is deciding where inventory sits versus where it is promised. Fourth, the mentorship line in his About suggests one people probe — answer it with planners grown into owners (S6's measurement handover; S8's drift flag owned by planning), not with org charts.

Three guessed LP focus areas, with rationale

Labeled guess. Amazon assigns 2–3 Leadership Principles per interviewer and does not disclose them. These three are inferred from the title, the JD and the public profile above (public-profile inference); if the first question lands on a different LP — Insist on the Highest Standards via the 99% bar, or Frugality via the capital releases — the twist maps under each story carry the pivot.

Answer strategy — business first, systems as enablers

  • Open with the process problem and its cost, not the system. First sentence names the broken process (Plan, Source, Make, Deliver or Return), who it hurt (customer, Finance, the floor) and the baseline number. The platform appears only as the enabler of the redesign, in one clause.
  • Use the JD's four outcome words as the results frame: reliability (did the promise hold?), responsiveness (how much earlier did we see it?), cost efficiency ($ with its basis label), asset utilization (capital, labor-hours, capacity released).
  • Action = operating-model redesign in gated phases — Discovery → Plan & Design → Build & Test → Cutover → Hypercare, each spoken as entry bar · what I delivered · exit bar. Say the gate that fired, not the meeting that happened.
  • Every dollar carries its basis word in the same breath — record · user anchor · modeled · priced. A record dollar is said flat; a modeled dollar is said with its inputs. Never promote a modeled number to record.
  • Program-lead voice. I owned definitions, rules, gates and the signature chain; engineers, SIs and vendor teams configured and built to the spec I signed. The only platform personally configured in canon is Manhattan DOM.
  • The pitch ends on the primary metric. Then stop. Silence after the pitch is the interviewer filling their STAR worksheet; their probes are the second half of the answer.
  • For Placement Analytics specifically: lead with the planning parameter that was wrong (brochure lead time; unfirmed orders counted as supply), the decision it corrupted (buffer size, shortage response), and the rule that released capital safely. Show one formula only when asked, labeled as the MIT toolkit illustration — the data team built the distributions; planners sized the buffers; Finance signed.
  • Answer style for this room: crisp, memo-like. Claim, evidence, stop. His background (Cornerstone rigor; a writing-heavy About line — public-profile inference) means a long wind-up reads as evasion; the one-breath basis label ("record", or "modeled, on illustrative inputs") does more credibility work here than any extra sentence.
  • Speak placement. Frame S6 as positioning safety stock — buffer placed only where measured forecast and lead-time errors justify it — and S8 as positioning the promise at p99 per lane, recomputed on a drift flag. His team decides where inventory sits versus where it is promised (public-profile inference); that is the exact shape of both stories.
  • Keep S6 and S8 apart: $2M (S6, forecast-driven, two-signal rule) and ~$1.5M / 580K units (S8, lead-time-driven, 99% bar) are different programs — never add them, never swap their mechanisms. "Six sites" — never "six NA sites"; never paste S6's "3 months" onto the six-site ingest (that is S7, not in this packet).
Loop-consistency rule. One question, one story; never the same story to two interviewers — interviewers compare notes. Held for other packets (do not spend here): S11/S12 (Tripathy) · S2/S18 (Goforth) · S1/S3 (Lan) · S16/S15/S10/S14 (Josyula). S7 (CM BOM) is an unallocated master-packet backup — pointer only, and no BOM-explosion content. S9, S19, S20, S21 and E7 are used here as primaries: if the same story is also drafted for another interviewer, tell it in one room only. If pushed for "another example" on an LP, use the named backup in the question bank or the backup bench at the end of this packet, never a story allocated to another interviewer.
How to read the "L6 level evidence" block under each story. Amazon's L6 functional bar (user-supplied rubric): Ambiguity — strategy or network undefined; I select the stakeholders, set the goals, deliver independently. Scope & influence — team-level scope; influences one to two levels up. Execution — tactical and strategic; simplifies or extends; applies best practices; force-multiplies; mitigates long-term risk; makes the short-versus-long-term trade-off explicit. Impact — long-term team goals. Process improvement — operational-excellence best practices institutionalized. Complex — visible risks, roadblocks and constraints mitigated through expertise, judgment and influence to consensus. Overview themes the block is tuned to: designing and modeling the supply chain rather than working in it; developing new processes for others to own; root-cause mechanisms that resolve trending issues at scale; recommendations to leadership that outline competing metrics. The block sits right after Results so the evidence is on the page when the probing starts.

The stories — business-first, canon numbers

Ten Elementum primaries from the same six-site manufacturer, the Walmart Marketplace client work and a new-market design engagement. Each primary: LPs carried · 20-second version · 60-second pitch → Situation/Task → Action in gated phases → Results → L6 evidence → the five facets → Probing with the four-table math → behavioral pack (four separate 90-second answers) → three angles → three trap drills. S6 and S8 are the existing stories, elevated; the other eight are new to this packet (red NEW pills mark every new section).

S6 — Elementum — one real-time supply/demand view across eight data domains: a shortage visible a lead-time early, forecast accuracy 70% → 85%, $2M of safety stock released under a two-signal rule ELEVATED

LPs this story can serve here: Dive Deep (the definition of supply; MAPE/bias at the decision grain) · Earn Trust (the two-signal release rule signed with Planning and Finance) · Deliver Results (third — alert live in 3 months) · Think Big (one view over eight domains) · Are Right, A Lot (backup — refusing unfirmed orders as supply)
Process family: Plan — S&OP / shortage management / safety-stock policy; data-integrity program behind the plan
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each NEW
  • Dive Deep — I profiled every domain and defined supply honestly before any screen — released planned orders only, because unfirmed MRP orders breathe every run.
  • Earn Trust — The $2M left the buffer only under a both-errors-improved rule Planning and Finance signed — the exception was written in before anyone asked.
  • Deliver Results — Forecast accuracy 70% → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs; the shortage alert live in three months; planner effort −50%; adoption 8×.
  • Ownership — Nobody owned the seam between eight data domains and the planners — I took the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery.
Honesty label. I owned the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery; the client's SAP teams and our integration engineers built the feeds; planners owned the forecast — I owned its measurement and the release rule. I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it. The safety-stock formula is how I explain it now with the MIT toolkit — an illustration, not a program artifact.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. A six-site manufacturer — mixed make-to-order and make-to-stock — learned about a component shortage when the line stopped. S&OP ran off private spreadsheets, shipment data was 60% complete, and supplier, material, BOM, inventory, production, transportation, sales-order and planning data lived in SAP and site systems but never met. Leadership's question — which customer orders are at risk this quarter? — had no data path, so the plan responded with expedites instead of re-plans and buffers sized on noise. Task. Nobody owned the seam; I took the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery. Action. I defined supply honestly before any screen — on-hand plus production plan plus released planned orders only, at SKU × location × date — and refused to count unfirmed MRP orders because they change every run. Domains went live in waves sequenced by planner trust, each behind a published data-quality gate, with daily reconciliation to the SAP source and false alarms worked as defects. The shortage alert fires when days-of-cover falls to the lane's measured p90 lead time — the last moment a re-plan is cheaper than a flight. Safety stock came out only under a rule Planning and Finance signed: release where forecast error and lead-time error had both improved. Result. Primary metric: forecast accuracy on the scoped class-A SKUs 70% → 85%. $2M of safety stock released under the two-signal rule — record, one-time working capital — with ≈$0.8M a year of modeled recurring value around it; the shortage alert live in three months; planner effort −50%. That $2M is a different program from the lane program's $1.5M; I never add them.

20-second version NEW

Eight data domains, one honest definition of supply, a shortage alert that fires when days-of-cover falls to the lane’s p90 lead time — a lead-time early — and $2M of safety stock released only where forecast error and lead-time error had both improved.

NACF bridge — one sentence

This is the NACF WOC loop: define supply honestly, alert at the last moment a re-plan is cheaper than an expedite, and cut weeks of cover only where both error signals have improved — INV-A and INV-B in the inbound booklet.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — A strategic manufacturing customer with six sites: planners learned about a component shortage when the line stopped; S&OP ran off private spreadsheets; shipment data was 60% complete; eight data domains lived in SAP and site systems but never met. Leadership's question — "which customer orders are at risk this quarter?" — had no data path.

The Plan process underneath was running on an unsafe definition of supply (unfirmed MRP orders counted because they looked better), an unmeasured lead-time error, and a shortage signal that arrived at line-down. The cost: expedites instead of re-plans, buffers sized on noise, and planners spending ~40% of their week reconciling rather than root-causing.

T — Nobody owned the seam — no spec, no agreed grain, no definition of "supply," no data-quality bar. I chose all four, as owner of the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery. Goal: a real-time supply/demand view with honest definitions and a shortage visible a lead-time early — and planner trust, which was the real product.

Baselines on the table: forecast accuracy 70% on scoped class-A SKUs · shortage detected at line-down (target: a lead-time early) · shipment-data completeness 60% · planner effort on reconciliation and firefighting (≈40% of the week, illustrative share; the −50% is record).

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (4–6 wks) — profiled every domain (completeness, latency, joinability) to my own gap templates; wrote the definitions memo — grain SKU × location × date; supply = on-hand + production plan + released planned orders only — and the metrics tree (OTIF, inventory $, MAPE/bias, lane lead-time accuracy, completeness, latency, adoption). Refused Planning's request to count unfirmed MRP orders as supply. Exit bar: signed gap findings per domain; scope frozen in writing; planners agree the supply definition before any screen.
  • Plan & Design (parallel) — master-data foundation plan; MPN mapping worklist with its coverage gate; domain-wave sequence by trust; DQ scorecard spec; the 10-step onboarding playbook. Exit bar: master-data gates green; unmapped-MPN list under threshold with a named owner; wave order signed by domain owners — who could fail their own wave.
  • Build & Test (8–12 wks) — domain waves behind published DQ gates — inventory and production first (numbers planners already believed), then purchasing/ASN, then transportation; shortage-alert rule specified (days-of-cover vs lane lead-time p90); daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52; weekly root-cause loop with false alarms worked as defects. Exit bar: per-domain completeness ≥ gate; reconciliation passing daily; false alarms trending down; pilot planners using the alert.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (4 wks) — views live for pilot planners with the at-risk list replacing the Monday spreadsheet; nobody mandated off the spreadsheet — rollback stayed free. Exit bar: planners answer "where is my product" from the platform in the live S&OP meeting.
  • Hypercare / Close (4 wks) — exception workbench staffed; weekly root-cause reviews handed to domain owners; the safety-stock release rule signed with Planning and Finance — release only where forecast error AND lead-time error both improved. Exit bar: blockers/day under threshold two consecutive weeks; the release survives audit.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the definitions memo, the metrics tree, the DQ scorecard spec, the wave sequence, the release rule and the onboarding playbook. The client's SAP teams and our integration engineers built the feeds; planners owned the forecast — I owned its measurement (MAPE/bias per SKU family) and the release rule; domain owners held sign-off over their own gates. The MPN worklist gated longer than planned — escalated to the sponsor as a re-sequenced date with the coverage number attached, not a slip.

Designed the process; others own it now. I designed the definitions, the DQ gates, the release rule and the onboarding playbook; domain owners now run the gates and the weekly root-cause reviews, and planners run the alert without me. Trade-off put to leadership. The recommendation to Planning and Finance outlined the competing metrics — working capital released vs service risk — and resolved it as a rule: release only where forecast error and lead-time error both improved.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (canon numbers only)
  • $ — $2M safety stock released (record, one-time working capital, under the both-errors-improved rule signed with Planning and Finance) + ≈$0.8M/yr modeled recurring (carry ≈$0.2M at 10% · expedites pre-empted ≈$0.3M · planner capacity ≈$0.3M; range $0.5–1.2M) — never added to S8's $1.5M.
  • # — eight data domains into one view across six sites; shortage-alert product live in 3 months; adoption 8× (record).
  • % — forecast accuracy 70% → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs (record); planner effort −50% (record); shipment data 60% complete at the start.
  • Time — the alert fires at days-of-cover ≤ lane lead-time p90 — a lead-time early instead of at line-down (record mechanism); views live in 3 months (the alert product; the six-site ingest is a separate 5-month program — never paste 3 months onto it).
  • Customer — "which customer orders are at risk this quarter?" answered from the platform in the live S&OP meeting, not a spreadsheet (record exit bar).
  • Quality — daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52; false alarms classified weekly to root cause and burned down as defects (record).

The five facets NEW — data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions; each labeled so the interviewer can see all five are present
1 · Data analysisData: eight domains (supplier, material, BOM, inventory, production, transportation, sales-order, planning) across six sites; shipment data 60% complete at the start; MAPE and bias at the SKU-family grain on scoped class-A SKUs. Revealed: unfirmed MRP orders inflated supply; the lane’s own lead time was unmeasured.
2 · Solution designOptions: count unfirmed orders or not · alert at brochure lead time or measured p90 · release buffer on accuracy alone or on both signals. Chosen: released planned orders only; alert at DoC ≤ lane p90; release only where both errors improved.
3 · Math & data logicMath: DoC = on-hand ÷ daily demand = 1,200 ÷ 100 = 12 ≤ p90 14 → alert; SS = z·d·σ_L = 2.33 × 100 × 4 = 932 → 466 at σ_L 2; alert precision 71.2% / recall 98.6% at p90; both-errors rule false-release rate under noise 25% → 2.5% with a one-SE bar.
4 · ImplementationImplementation: definitions memo → data-quality gates per domain → waves sequenced by planner trust → daily reconciliation to SAP source → alert live in 3 months → release rule signed → false alarms classified weekly.
5 · Probe questionsProbe questions: MAPE vs bias · what “accuracy” means · why p90 for the alert and p99 for the promise · false alarms · whether “both improved” is signal or noise — see Trap Drills.
Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer

Analytics — How was 70 → 85% measured, and on what?

Accuracy = 1 − MAPE on the scoped class-A SKUs, with bias published alongside it from month two, at the SKU-family grain the planners decide on. The planners owned the forecast; I owned the measurement and the input integrity — I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it.

Analytics — Why exclude unfirmed MRP planned orders from supply? That understates it.

Deliberately. Unfirmed orders breathe every MRP run; counting them amplifies noise and inflates supply, which is exactly how the line-down surprises happened. Released planned orders only — accepted by planners before the first screen was built.

Analytics — The $2M — how do I know it is not a prettier average?

Because of the release rule: buffer came out only on SKU families where forecast error and lead-time error had both demonstrably improved, signed by Planning and Finance. That is why the release survived audit. It is one-time working capital; the recurring value around it is my model and labeled.

Analytics — Your expedite row is the softest. Defend it.

Agreed — about sixty expedites a year pre-empted at a $5K premium, both illustrative. The record is the mechanism: the alert fires at days-of-cover ≤ lane p90. To firm it I would pull the expedited-freight ledger for the six sites twelve months before and after the alert went live and count the premium directly.

Analytics — Why p90 for the alert threshold and p99 for the promise (S8)?

Different decisions. The alert is a re-plan trigger — p90 gives the planner a window before the tail bites. The promise is a commitment — it sits at the tail you can defend. Same measured distribution, two policies read off it.

Analytics — Define your two error metrics: MAPE versus bias. Which one steered?

MAPE is mean absolute percentage error — how wrong the forecast is on average, direction ignored; bias is the signed mean error — whether it runs systematically high or low. MAPE without bias hides a chronic over-forecast inside a decent average, and a chronic over-forecast is exactly how buffer quietly accumulates. So accuracy was reported as 1 − MAPE on the scoped class-A SKUs at the SKU-family grain, with bias published alongside from month two — and the release rule required forecast error and lead-time error both to improve, so neither metric could carry the decision alone.

Analytics — What are the precision and recall of an alert at the lane’s p90?

On an illustrative lane — replenishment time normal with mean 10 and σ 3 days, so p90 = 13.8 — and days-of-cover states uniform across 0–120 days: of every 1,000 checks about 83 would run short without action; the p90 alert fires on about 115; 71.2% of alerts are real (precision) and 98.6% of shortages are caught (recall). Moving to p99 lifts recall to 99.9% and drops precision to 58.8%. Illustrative lane; the record is the rule, DoC ≤ lane p90.

Analytics — Two kinds of false alarm — how do you tell them apart?

Statistical and defect. A designed false positive is an alert on a lane that turned out fine — at p90 about 29% of alerts by construction; that is the price of the window. A defect false alarm is the data lying — a wrong on-hand, a missing receipt, an unfirmed order counted as supply. The record burns the second kind down weekly by root cause; I do not let the first kind masquerade as a data problem.

Analytics — Is “both errors improved” signal or noise?

Under no real improvement each metric is a coin flip, so both improve by chance 25% of the time — on 100 families, about 25 false releases. Requiring each to beat its own standard error cuts that to 2.5% (about 2.5 families); two standard errors, 0.05%. The record says “demonstrably improved”; how that was operationalized is a number I would confirm before I quote one. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH

Analytics — Why does bias matter when MAPE is already 15%?

Because direction compounds. A +10% over-forecast on 100 units a day adds 70 units a week; in 13.3 weeks the excess alone equals the whole 932-unit buffer on the example lane. MAPE tells you how far off; bias tells you which way the stock is piling up.

Shadow / closing — What would you do differently?

Start the MPN-mapping worklist on day one — it is never "done" and it gated more than I planned. And define supply honestly before any screen, which held.

Where this lands at Amazon. Placement rests on two error signals — forecast error and lead-time error — measured at the decision grain. Alert-threshold design — precision, recall and the cost of a false alarm (extra table)

The record is the rule — alert when days-of-cover ≤ the lane’s measured p90 lead time. Everything below is my after-the-fact design arithmetic on an illustrative lane, so the threshold can be defended in numbers. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH how p90 was originally chosen.

StepQuantityFormula → substitutionInputs (basis)Value
1The lanereplenishment time L ~ Normal(μ = 10, σ = 3); p90 = 10 + 1.2816 × 3 = 13.84 ≈ 14 dillustrative lane (matches the p90 = 14 example)θ = 13.84 d
2Shortage if no actionP(L > DoC) = 1 − Φ((DoC − 10)/3); DoC uniform on 0–120 d → ≈ 10/120 = 8.3% of checksstates illustrative; ∫P(L > x)dx = E[L] = 1083.3 per 1,000 checks
3Alert rule (record)alert iff DoC ≤ θ: alerts per 1,000 = 1,000 × 13.84 ÷ 120 = 115record design; values illustrative115 alerts / 1,000
4Precision (p90)TP ÷ alerts = 82.15 ÷ 115.37 = 71.2%numerical integral of P(L > DoC) over DoC ≤ θ71.2%
5Recall (p90)TP ÷ shortages = 82.15 ÷ 83.34 = 98.6%same integral98.6%
6Same lane at p99θ = 16.98 d; alerts 141; precision 58.8%; recall 99.9%same methodmore alerts, little recall gained
7Cost of a false alarm vs a missplanner hour ≈ $120K ÷ 2,000 h = $60; a missed shortage ≈ $5,000 (master expedite premium)both illustrative (master)$60 vs $5,000
8Expected cost per 1,000 checksp75 $24,656; p90 115.372 × $60 + 1.1836 × $5,000 = $12,840; p95 $10,079; p99 $8,913alerts × c_fp + misses × c_missp90 $12,840 vs p99 $8,913
9Cost-optimal thresholdalert iff P(L > DoC) ≥ c_fp ÷ c_miss = 60 ÷ 5,000 = 1.2% → quantile 98.8% → θ = 16.8 dcritical-ratio logicθ* = 16.8 d if c_miss were $5,000
10What p90 impliesp90 ⇔ c_miss ÷ c_fp = 0.90 ÷ 0.10 = 9 → c_miss = 9 × $60 = $540; p99 ⇔ $5,940after-the-fact derivation — not how p90 was chosenp90 prices a miss at $540
11Minimum shipments to bound the p90n = 31: t(24)…t(31); n = 30 → t(24)…openexact order-statistic interval (95%)n ≥ 31 per lane; else cohort p90
Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured number is two million dollars of safety stock released, and that is record, signed with planning and Finance under the both-errors-improved rule. Around it I model roughly eight hundred thousand a year of recurring value: about two hundred thousand in carry cost on the capital that came out, about three hundred thousand in expedite premium the shortage alert pre-empts because it fires a lead-time early instead of at line-down, and about three hundred thousand of planner capacity from the fifty percent effort cut. The two million and the fifty percent are measured; the three recurring pieces are my model on benchmark rates, and I keep them separate from the one-and-a-half million in the lane program."

If challenged — the full answers.

The expedite figure is the softest

It rests on about sixty expedites a year pre-empted at a five thousand dollar premium, both illustrative; to firm it I would pull the expedited-freight ledger for the six sites for the twelve months before and after the alert went live and count the premium directly.

Planner capacity is not cash

Agreed, it is two point four FTE of time redeployed, not headcount removed; the fifty percent is measured, the forty percent share of the week is my assumption, and a two-week time study on the S&OP prep cycle would replace it.

Why not claim the whole two million every year

Because a release is a one-time working-capital event; the recurring value is the carry at eight to twelve percent, and I would rather say two hundred thousand a year honestly than two million a year wrongly.

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you dug into the data to find the real problem. Dive Deep

Open (first two sentences): Leadership asked which customer orders were at risk, and the first thing I did was refuse to answer until we agreed what “supply” meant.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a six-site manufacturer found out about shortages when the line stopped; shipment data was 60% complete across eight domains. Task: nobody owned the seam, so I took definitions and data integrity. Action: profiled every domain for completeness, latency and joinability, and defined supply honestly — on-hand plus production plan plus released planned orders only; unfirmed MRP orders change every run and had been inflating supply. Result: the alert product live in three months; forecast accuracy 70 to 85% on the scoped class-A SKUs; planners accepted the definition before the first screen existed.

Land this number: 60% shipment-data completeness → daily reconciliation to SAP; 70% → 85% accuracy. · Pivot to NACF: NACF inbound “available” and “on-hand” need one signed definition before any placement model reads them. · Do NOT say: Do not say I owned the forecast; planners did — I owned the measurement and the input integrity.

Q2 Tell me about a time you needed buy-in from Finance and Planning on a risky change. Earn Trust

Open (first two sentences): Releasing $2M of safety stock is a risk Finance and Planning both carry, so the rule was theirs before it was mine.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: buffer had grown on noisy forecasts and unmeasured lead times. Task: take capital out without a stockout. Action: I proposed a two-signal rule — release only where forecast error and lead-time error had both demonstrably improved — and got Planning and Finance to sign it before the first release. Result: $2M released and it survived audit because the exception was in the rule; ≈$0.8M a year modeled around it; never added to the other program’s $1.5M.

Land this number: $2M released (record, one-time); both-errors rule. · Pivot to NACF: A WOC reduction at Amazon needs the same pre-signed exception: cut only where both signals improved. · Do NOT say: Do not add $2M and $1.5M, and do not call the $0.8M recurring a result — it is modeled.

Q3 Tell me about a time you delivered a result on a hard deadline. Deliver Results

Open (first two sentences): Three months from kickoff, a planner could see a shortage a lead time before the line stopped.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: the plan responded with expedites, not re-plans. Task: ship a shortage alert fast without shipping wrong data. Action: sequenced the domains in waves by planner trust, each behind a published data-quality gate, with daily reconciliation to SAP and false alarms worked as defects. Result: alert live in three months, planner effort −50%, adoption 8×, forecast accuracy 70 to 85%. The six-site ingest is a separate five-month program — I never paste the three months onto it.

Land this number: Alert live in 3 months; planner effort −50%; adoption 8×. · Pivot to NACF: Same sequencing for NACF: trust-ordered waves, a quality gate per source. · Do NOT say: Do not stretch “3 months” to the full ingest.

Q4 Tell me about a time you owned something nobody owned. Ownership

Open (first two sentences): Eight domains, six sites, and the question “which customer orders are at risk?” had no data path and no owner.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: S&OP ran off private spreadsheets and the seam between systems and planners belonged to nobody. Task: own it. Action: I wrote the definitions memo, the data-quality scorecard spec, the wave sequence and the release rule, and stayed through hypercare. The thing I would do differently: start the MPN-mapping worklist on day one — it is never done and it gated more than I planned. Result: the question was answered in the live S&OP meeting from the platform, not a spreadsheet.

Land this number: Eight domains into one view; alert in 3 months. · Pivot to NACF: Placement analytics lives on that seam too: forecast error and lead-time error from different systems. · Do NOT say: Do not claim the client’s SAP teams’ feeds were mine.

Trap drills NEW — the three nastiest follow-ups a statistically rigorous interviewer throws

T1 “Your alert fires at the lane’s p90 — what are its precision and recall, and why not p99?”

The numbers: Illustrative lane N(10, 3): θ = 13.84; precision 71.2%, recall 98.6%; p99 precision 58.8%, recall 99.9%; p90 ⇔ miss costs 9× a false alarm.

Say exactly: “On an illustrative lane the p90 alert catches about 99 percent of shortages with about 71 percent precision; p99 buys about one more point of recall for twelve points of precision. A p90 threshold prices a miss at nine false alarms — at a sixty-dollar alert and a five-thousand-dollar miss the cost-optimal threshold would sit higher, which is why I would show planners the curve and let their own cost of an alert set the dial.”

T2 “Both-errors-improved — what is the false-release rate if neither metric really improved?”

The numbers: Coin-flip: 25% of families; each beat by one SE: 2.52%; two SE: 0.052%; on 100 families: 25 / 2.5 / 0.05.

Say exactly: “If nothing improved, a bare both-up rule releases a quarter of the families by luck; requiring each metric to beat its own standard error takes it to about two and a half percent — which is why the rule said demonstrably improved, not merely improved.”

T3 “Why does bias matter if your accuracy is 85%?”

The numbers: SS = 2.33 × 100 × 4 = 932; +10% bias × 100/day × 7 = 70/week; 932 ÷ 70 = 13.3 weeks.

Say exactly: “Accuracy is a magnitude; bias is a direction. A ten-percent over-forecast adds seventy units a week on a hundred-a-day SKU — in under fourteen weeks the excess alone equals a nine-hundred-unit buffer, which is how buffer quietly accumulates behind a decent MAPE.”

Process family: Plan / Source / Deliver seam — inbound lead-time policy, safety-stock sizing, promise policy; parameter governance with a drift flag
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each NEW
  • Dive Deep — I measured each lane’s own distribution — mean, spread, tail — instead of accepting a carrier brochure or a network average.
  • Customer Obsession — The customer was the production line: I put the cost of a line-down next to the carrying cost per lane and let planning choose p99 with the asymmetry in front of them.
  • Earn Trust — Finance signed each lane; the release rule carried its own exception — fat tails kept their buffer — before anyone asked.
  • Ownership — I designed the promise policy and release rule as a signed planning act that planning re-applies on its own refresh.
Honesty label. I owned the requirement (measured lanes, not brochures), the p99 promise policy and the release rule; the data team built the distributions; planners sized the buffers; Finance signed the release. Lane-cohort counts and week markers are not in the record. The safety-stock formula is the MIT inventory-theory toolkit as I explain it now — an illustration, not a program artifact. 60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. The same six-site manufacturer planned inventory on carrier-brochure lead times — every buffer sized on a variability nobody had measured: too much on reliable lanes, too little on fat-tailed ones. The logistics manager asked for a "lead-time variability report." Task. I reframed it as a parameter and policy problem — nobody owned the seam between transportation reality and planning parameters, and I took it — scoped at 200 lanes by site, lane and mode. Action. I wrote the requirement to the data team: mean, spread and tail per lane from actual production cycle plus inbound transit; defined the promise at p99 cumulatively — the smallest day d with P(L ≤ d) ≥ 0.99, not average-plus-padding — and showed each lane's asymmetry, line-down cost against carrying cost, and let planning choose the percentile; they chose p99. The release rule was written as a signed planning act with the exception inside it: fat-tail lanes keep their buffer until the source process moves; Finance agreed the rule in the same room, before any cut. I would not endorse a release until lead-time accuracy hit 99% on the 200-lane set; the stockout-and-expedite watch was armed first; every parameter got a drift flag and a refresh cadence so the promise never decays into a new brochure. Result. Primary metric: lead-time accuracy on 200 lanes — brochure values → 99% at the p99 promise. 580K units, about $1.5M of safety stock, released without a stockout — record, one-time working capital — with ≈$0.6M a year modeled around it. A different program from the $2M forecast release; never added.

20-second version NEW

Two hundred lanes planned on carrier brochures; I replaced them with measured per-lane distributions, set the promise at p99, gated any cut on 99% lead-time accuracy and released 580K units, about $1.5M, with zero stockouts.

NACF bridge — one sentence

Inbound lead-time variability is half of every WOC target and every placement promise at NACF — I would measure each vendor lane’s own distribution and promise at the tail I can defend before releasing any weeks of cover.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — The six-site manufacturer's logistics manager asked for a "lead-time variability report." The real problem: inventory planning ran on carrier-brochure lead times — every buffer sized on a variability nobody had measured; too much buffer on reliable lanes, too little on fat-tailed ones; the promise to the plan was folklore.

The risk cut both ways: keep planning on brochures and you hold too much on reliable lanes and stock out on fat-tailed ones; cut buffer without evidence and the stockout is yours. And an unmaintained promise decays into a new brochure — which is why every parameter needed a drift flag.

T — Nobody owned the seam between transportation reality and planning parameters; I took it. Goal: measured lanes replace catalog values; a promise at a defensible tail; buffer released only on evidence, with service held — scoped at 200 lanes by site, lane and mode.

Baselines on the table: lead-time parameters = carrier-brochure values, variability unmeasured · buffer capital sized on catalog values · no lead-time-accuracy measurement · target 99% lead-time accuracy on the 200-lane set before any release.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — reframed the report request as a parameter problem; scoped 200 lanes by site/lane/mode; wrote the success criteria — measured lanes replace catalog values; buffer released only on evidence; fat tails keep buffer; showed one right-skewed lane where the average hid the tail. Exit bar: planning and logistics agree the problem is the parameter, not the report.
  • Plan & Design — requirement to the data team (mean, spread, tail per lane; production cycle + inbound transit vs actual); the p99 promise policy (cumulative, not μ + 2.33σ); the release rule as a signed planning act with the fat-tail exception inside it; the stockout-and-expedite watch; drift flag and refresh cadence. On p95 vs p99, showed the asymmetry per lane and left the choice to planning. Exit bar: policy signed by planning; Finance agrees the release rule in the room; data team accepts the spec.
  • Build & Test — data team built the lane distributions and tracked lead-time accuracy per lane; manual reconciliation stayed alive. Exit bar: lead-time accuracy 99% on the 200-lane set — the bar I would not endorse a cut below.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — planners executed the buffer release lane by lane under the signed rule; fat-tail lanes excluded until their source process moved; watch armed first. Exit bar: 580K units / ~$1.5M released; no stockout in the watch window.
  • Hypercare / Close — read the watch; set drift flags per lane; handed the refresh cadence and the release rule to planning as an act they could re-apply. Exit bar: watch clean; the promise stays measured.

Influence and ownership. I owned the requirement, the promise policy and the release rule; the data team built the distributions and tracked accuracy; planners sized the buffers and executed; Finance signed each lane. When Finance pushed for a broader release, the rule they had signed did the refusing — fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves. The percentile fight never happened: I showed each lane's line-down cost against carrying cost and planning chose p99 — their choice surviving an audit is worth more than my choice winning a meeting.

Designed the process; others own it now. I designed the promise policy and the release rule as a signed planning act; planning now re-applies it on its own refresh cadence with a drift flag per lane. Trade-off put to leadership. The recommendation to Finance and Planning outlined the competing metrics — ~$1.5M of working capital out vs stockout exposure at the tail — and set the bar: p99, 99% lead-time accuracy, fat-tail lanes excluded until their source process moved.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (canon numbers only)
  • $ — 580K units / ~$1.5M of safety stock released without a stockout (record, one-time working capital); ≈$0.6M/yr modeled recurring around it (carry ≈$0.3M at a benchmark 20% all-in rate + expedites avoided ≈$0.3M; range $0.3–1.4M) — a different program from S6's $2M; never added.
  • # — 200 lanes measured by site, lane and mode; 580K units released (record); ≈$2.59/unit derived — low-value components.
  • % — lead-time accuracy 99% on the 200-lane set at the p99 promise — the bar below which I would not endorse a cut (record).
  • Time — release only after the 99% bar; watch window clean; refresh cadence and drift flag per lane handed to planning (record).
  • Customer — service held — zero stockouts on released lanes through the watch window (record).
  • Quality — release only under the signed rule; fat-tail lanes kept buffer until the source process moved; the promise stays measured, never a new brochure (record).

The five facets NEW — data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions; each labeled so the interviewer can see all five are present
1 · Data analysisData: shipment durations per lane (ship-to-receipt) for 200 lanes by site, lane and mode; the brochure value beside each. Revealed: lanes with measured σ far below the brochure (release) and lanes above it (keep).
2 · Solution designOptions: brochure · average · p95 · p99 per lane · network-wide percentile. Chosen: promise at the lane’s own p99, cut only behind a 99% accuracy gate, fat tails kept.
3 · Math & data logicMath: SS = z·d·σ_L = 2.33 × 500 × 5 = 5,825 vs 2.33 × 500 × 1.2 = 1,398; release 4,427; p99 = min{d : P(L ≤ d) ≥ 0.99}; $1.5M ÷ 580K = $2.59; upper-bound σ at n = 50 → 1,675.
4 · ImplementationImplementation: reframe the request → scope 200 lanes → data team builds distributions → planning chooses p99 on the asymmetry → accuracy gate → lane-by-lane release with Finance signature → watch + drift flag.
5 · Probe questionsProbe questions: p99 vs p95 · skew and the normal shortcut · what “99% accuracy” means · whether the release is selection-biased · whether the $1.5M is measured — see Trap Drills.
Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer

Analytics — Why p99 and not p95? That is a lot of buffer.

Per lane, the cost of a line-down on a fat-tailed lane dwarfed the carrying cost of the extra day. I showed the asymmetry lane by lane and let planning choose — they chose p99. On reliable lanes the measured σ was so far below the brochure that p99 still released buffer; on fat-tailed lanes the buffer stayed.

Analytics — How did you compute p99 on skewed lanes?

Cumulatively from the empirical distribution — the smallest d with P(L ≤ d) ≥ 0.99 — not μ + 2.33σ. On one right-skewed lane the normal shortcut understated the true p99 by about 3.4 days; that lane is why the definition is cumulative.

Analytics — Is the $1.5M measured?

The release is record: 580K units out, zero stockouts in the watch window, Finance signed each lane. The ≈$2.59/unit is the derived average. What is modeled is the recurring value around it — carry at 20% all-in (capital-only would be ≈$150K at 10%) and avoided expedites — each input labeled.

Analytics — The expedite number assumes what?

Ten thousand shipments a year on the 200 lanes and one late arrival in ten forcing an expedite before p99 — illustrative. I would pull the expedite ledger by lane for the year before and after and compare directly.

Analytics — What is lead-time accuracy, exactly?

1 − |actual − promised| / promised, tracked per lane against the p99 promise; the bar was 99% on the whole 200-lane set. Below it, no release — written into the act before anyone asked.

Analytics — At what grain did you measure lead time, and over what window?

At the lane grain, from actual ship-to-receipt durations — one empirical distribution per lane, never a network average. The record carries the per-lane distributions, the refresh cadence and the drift flag (p99 recomputed on refresh; the buffer decision re-opens if accuracy falls); it does not carry a quoted window length, and I will not invent one — what mattered design-wise was a window long enough to hold seasonality and recent enough that a dead lane's history could not vote, with the drift flag as the safety net either way.

Analytics — Why p99? What cost ratio does that imply?

A p99 promise is the critical ratio 0.99 = Cu ÷ (Cu + Co), i.e. a late day costs 99 times an extra day of cover. On the example lane an extra day of cover is 500 × $2.59 × 20% ÷ 365 = $0.71, so p99 is justified if a day of shortage costs more than about $70; p95 if more than $13. A line-down clears both bars by orders of magnitude — which is the asymmetry I showed planning lane by lane.

Analytics — Lanes released are lanes with a small measured σ. Isn’t that selection bias?

Yes — the winner’s curse. A lane picked because its measured σ looks small is partly a lucky draw. The standard error on σ is about σ ÷ √(2(n−1)): 10.1% at n = 50, 3.5% at n = 400. On the reliable lane (σ 1.2, brochure 5), sizing on the 95% upper bound at n = 50 gives σ = 1.44 and a buffer of 1,675 instead of 1,398 — 277 units more, a release of 4,150 instead of 4,427. That is the arithmetic; VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH what the program did on thin lanes.

Analytics — Define “99% lead-time accuracy” so I can test it.

The master’s definition is LTacc = 1 − |actual − promised| ÷ promised with a 99% bar on the 200-lane set. Read per shipment against a p99 promise it cannot be 99%: on the example lane the mean shipment is 21 days against a 34-day promise, |21 − 34| ÷ 34 = 38.2%, a score of 61.8%. The readings consistent with 99% are coverage — 99 of 100 shipments arrive within the promise — or the re-measured lane p99 within 1% of the promised p99, a tolerance of 0.34 day on a 34-day lane. On the ratio form 1% is 0.05 day on a 5-day lane and 0.3 day on a 30-day lane. I would say which reading before quoting a tolerance. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH

Shadow / closing — How does the promise stay honest after you leave?

Drift flag per lane with a refresh cadence: p99 is recomputed on refresh, and the buffer decision re-opens if accuracy falls. The stockout-and-expedite watch became a standing report owned by planning.

Where this lands at Amazon. Inbound lead-time variability is half of every buffer and every promise in a placement model. 5Total recurring0.3 + 0.3 = $0.6Mmodeled; never added to S6's $2M≈$0.6M/yr

Release-decision design — point estimate vs upper bound; what “99% accuracy” can mean (extra table)

The record is the rule — measured per-lane distribution, p99 promise, 99% accuracy gate, fat tails kept, Finance signs. The statistics below are my design arithmetic on illustrative lanes. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH what the program did on thin lanes and which accuracy definition it used.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured result is five hundred eighty thousand units, about a million and a half dollars of safety stock, released at ninety-nine percent lead-time accuracy with zero stockouts on the watch; that is record and Finance signed it. What recurs around it I model at roughly six hundred thousand a year: about three hundred thousand in carrying cost on capital we no longer hold, and about three hundred thousand in expedites we no longer pay because the promise sits at the ninety-ninth percentile instead of a brochure number. The release and the accuracy are measured; the two recurring pieces are my model on benchmark carrying and expedite rates, and I never add this to the two million from the forecast program."

If challenged — the full answers.

The expedite number is the softest

It assumes ten thousand shipments a year on the two hundred lanes and that one late arrival in ten forced an expedite before p99; to firm it I would pull the expedite ledger by lane for the year before and after the release and compare directly.

Twenty percent carrying cost is high

Fifteen to twenty-five is the textbook range and I use the middle; capital alone is eight to twelve, so if Finance wants capital only the carry is about a hundred and fifty thousand and I would say that instead.

Why is the million and a half not an annual saving

Because it is a one-time working-capital release, booked once as a signed planning act; what recurs is the carry and the avoided expedites, and I would rather be right at six hundred thousand a year than wrong at a million and a half.

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you went deeper than the request. Dive Deep

Open (first two sentences): The ask was a “lead-time variability report.” I turned it into a parameter question: what number is the plan actually using, and how wrong is it?

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a six-site manufacturer’s logistics manager wanted a report; inventory was sized on carrier-brochure lead times with variability unmeasured. Task: decide whether a report was the deliverable. Action: I reframed it as a parameter problem, scoped 200 lanes by site, lane and mode, wrote the success criteria — measured lanes replace brochures, and 99% lead-time accuracy before any cut — and had the data team build per-lane distributions. Result: planning chose a p99 promise and 580K units, about $1.5M, came out with zero stockouts.

Land this number: 200 lanes; 99% lead-time accuracy; 580K units / ~$1.5M, 0 stockouts. · Pivot to NACF: NACF inbound lead-time variability by vendor lane is the same “report” that is really a parameter. · Do NOT say: Do not say the data team’s distributions were mine; I owned the requirement, the policy and the release rule.

Q2 Tell me about a time you put the customer’s cost ahead of your own team’s metric. Customer Obsession

Open (first two sentences): The customer was the production line. A released unit of buffer that causes a line-down is worse than a unit never released.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: carrying cost pushed to cut buffer; the line-down cost sat in another budget. Task: make the asymmetry visible. Action: I showed it lane by lane — an extra day of cover on the example lane costs about $0.71; a day of shortage costs far more — and let planning choose the quantile; they chose p99. On reliable lanes p99 still released buffer; on fat-tail lanes the buffer stayed. Result: zero stockouts across the released lanes.

Land this number: Zero stockouts (record); p99 on 200 lanes. · Pivot to NACF: Placement trades FC buffer cost against a missed promise the same way. · Do NOT say: Do not say planning was forced — say they chose with the asymmetry in front of them.

Q3 Tell me about a time you got a finance partner to sign a risky decision. Earn Trust

Open (first two sentences): Finance signed each lane because the exception was written into the rule before anyone asked.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: releasing safety stock is irreversible in practice. Task: a rule Finance could audit. Action: below 99% lead-time accuracy, no release; fat tails keep their buffer; Finance signs per lane; a stockout-and-expedite watch from day one. Result: $1.5M of working capital released, Finance-signed, no stockout in the watch window.

Land this number: 580K units / ~$1.5M (record, one-time). · Pivot to NACF: NACF WOC changes need a pre-signed exception clause too. · Do NOT say: Do not call the $0.6M recurring a result — it is modeled.

Q4 Tell me about a process you designed that someone else now owns. Ownership

Open (first two sentences): I designed the promise policy and the release rule as a signed planning act; planning re-applies it on its own refresh.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a one-off analysis decays into a new brochure. Task: leave a process, not a project. Action: drift flag per lane — p99 recomputed on refresh, the buffer decision re-opens if accuracy falls — and the watch became a standing report owned by planning. Result: the promise stays honest after I leave.

Land this number: Drift flag per lane; standing report owned by planning. · Pivot to NACF: Every NACF parameter I touch gets a drift flag and an owner. · Do NOT say: Do not say I run it today.

  • Winner’s curse on thin lanes: size on the upper bound.
  • Accuracy definition: coverage or p99-match.

Trap drills NEW — the three nastiest follow-ups a statistically rigorous interviewer throws

T1 “Define 99% lead-time accuracy — what tolerance does it allow?”

The numbers: Ratio form: 1% × 5 d = 0.05 d; 1% × 30 d = 0.3 d. Per shipment vs a p99 promise: |21 − 34| ÷ 34 = 38.2% → 61.8%. Coverage form: 1 shipment in 100 beyond the promise.

Say exactly: “Written as one minus the absolute miss over the promise, one percent is a twentieth of a day on a five-day lane and a third of a day on a thirty-day lane — but against a p99 promise the average shipment misses by about thirty-eight percent, so I read ninety-nine as coverage: ninety-nine of a hundred shipments inside the promise. I would confirm which definition the record uses before I quote a tolerance.”

T2 “Why p99 and not p95 — what is that buffer worth?”

The numbers: Carry per day of cover = 500 × $2.59 × 20% ÷ 365 = $0.71; p99 ⇔ a shortage-day worth > 99 × = $70; p95 ⇔ > $13.

Say exactly: “A p99 promise says a shortage day costs ninety-nine carrying days — about seventy dollars on that lane — and a line-down clears that by orders of magnitude; I showed planning the asymmetry lane by lane and they chose p99.”

T3 “You released the lanes with the smallest measured σ. Isn’t that a selection effect?”

The numbers: σ̂ standard error = σ ÷ √(2(n−1)) = 10.1% at n = 50; upper-bound σ 1.44; buffer 1,675 vs 1,398; 277 units held back.

Say exactly: “Yes, partly — a lane picked for a small measured spread is partly a lucky draw; on a fifty-shipment lane sizing on the upper bound holds back about two hundred seventy-seven units of the four thousand four hundred I would otherwise release. I would show that adjustment next to every release.”

S8a — Elementum — actual-vs-planned lead-time distribution analysis: the brochure was wrong three ways — 3 days short on the mean, twice-too-wide on the spread, 16 days short on the tail NEW

LPs this story serves here: Dive Deep (primary — plan vs actual at the shipment grain) · Are Right, A Lot (rank, don’t fit) · Insist on the Highest Standards (a “99%” plan that held 96.5% is not a 99% plan) · Earn Trust (the carrier’s own scans, not my opinion)
Process family: Plan / Source seam — inbound lead-time parameter diagnosis before any buffer or policy change
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each NEW
  • Dive Deep — I compared plan to actual at the shipment grain on three axes — location, spread, tail — before changing a single parameter; 240 of 400 shipments ran slower than the brochure.
  • Are Right, A Lot — I ranked the shipments instead of fitting a bell curve; the curve’s “99%” plan held 96.5% — 14 of 400 shipments ran past it — and the ranked p99 held 99%.
  • Insist on the Highest Standards — I refused to publish a p99 that carried its own error: a plan statistic had to be distribution-free and come with its sampling uncertainty.
  • Earn Trust — I showed planners and commercial the carrier’s own scans on one lane — brochure 18, mean 21, p99 34 — so the argument was the data, not my say-so.
Honesty label. I owned the question, the comparison design and the plan statistic; the data team built the per-lane distributions to my requirement. The 18 / 21 / σ 4 / p99 34 / n≈400 lane is the loop packet’s example lane (secondary source — the master packet carries “18d brochure” in its crosswalk and “lane-cohort counts are not in the record”); the ERP default σ of 8 days and the 7-day manual pad are that packet’s illustrative inputs. Say “illustrative inputs, real outcomes”: the record is 200 lanes, 99% lead-time accuracy at the p99 promise, 580K units / ~$1.5M released. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH that the example-lane numbers are the ones you are comfortable quoting.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. The six-site manufacturer’s safety stock kept growing and nobody could say why. Planning ran on carrier-brochure lead times — on my example lane, 18 days — plus a manual week of pad that every planner added. Task. Before touching a parameter I owned one question: how, exactly, was the planned lead time wrong? Action. I pulled about a year of shipment history on the lane — roughly 400 shipments — and compared plan to actual three ways. Location: the actual mean was 21 days, three days longer than the brochure, fifteen standard errors — not noise — and 240 of 400 shipments, 60%, ran slower than the number in the system. Spread: the ERP’s default assumed an 8-day standard deviation; measured, it was 4 — wrong in the opposite direction, which is why planners had padded by hand. Tail: a bell curve from that mean and spread puts the 99th percentile at 30.3 days, but 14 of the 400 shipments ran longer — a “99%” plan that actually held 96.5%. Ranking the shipments puts it at 34. Result. Primary metric: shipments late against the plan on that lane, 60% → 1%, planned lead time 18 → 34 days; at program level, lead-time accuracy 99% on 200 lanes — record. The example lane is the pattern, not the headline.

20-second version NEW

The planned lead time was wrong in three directions at once — the mean was 3 days short, the ERP’s spread was twice the real one, and the tail was 16 days out; I measured all three on the carrier’s own scans and planned to the ranked p99, not a bell curve.

NACF bridge — one sentence

Every NACF inbound plan is a promised date; I would run the same plan-vs-actual on appointment and ASN dates versus receive scans, by vendor lane, before anyone resizes a buffer — location, spread, tail.

Situation / Task — the problem space

S — Elementum, the same six-site manufacturer. Buffers kept growing, service was still shaky and nobody could explain why. Inventory planning ran on carrier-brochure lead times (18 days on the example lane) and every planner added an informal week of pad. The risk of doing nothing: working capital grows every quarter and every stockout triggers more padding — a wrong number plus panic.

T — Own the diagnosis: characterize how the planned lead time was wrong — bias, spread, tail — with the uncertainty on each statement, so the later policy (S8) and the formula redesign (S8b) stand on measured facts. Scope: 200 lanes by site, lane and mode; the example lane is the busy one.

Baselines on the table: planned lead time = brochure (18 d on the example lane) · actual mean 21 d · 60% of shipments slower than plan · ERP default σ_LT = 8 d (never measured) · manual pad 7 d × 500 units/day = 3,500 units on the lane · no lead-time-accuracy measurement existed.

Action — five gated phases
  • Discovery — agreed with planning and logistics that the question was “how is the parameter wrong,” not “write a variability report”; chose the example lane because it had the history (≈8 shipments a week for a year). Exit bar: the comparison design — three tests, one lane — accepted before any number was shown.
  • Plan & Design — requirement to the data team: promised date (PO-line schedule) vs actual receipt per shipment, keyed by lane = site × ship-from × mode, 12+ months so every season is in the sample; outputs mean, σ, p50 / p90 / p99, n and the late share against the plan in force. Exit bar: data team accepts the spec; planning agrees which plan statistic is on trial (mean, brochure + pad, normal p99, ranked p99).
  • Build & Test — data team built the lane file; I owned the comparison: bias with its standard error, late share with a binomial interval, σ against the ERP default, then the four candidate plans scored on the same 400 shipments. Exit bar: the candidate table reproduces on a second pull; the ranked p99 reproduces the “99%” by construction and its sampling interval is printed beside it.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — the finding went to planners as one lane, three numbers — brochure 18, mean 21, p99 34 — then the same three tests per lane for all 200; the plan statistic moved to the ranked p99 under the release rule (S8) and the formula (S8b). Exit bar: planning signs the plan statistic; the old brochure stays in the master as a labeled reference only.
  • Hypercare / Close — drift flag per lane (p99 recomputed on refresh; review if the tail or the spread moves) and a standing plan-vs-actual page owned by planning. Exit bar: the page refreshes without me; thin lanes carry their interval on the page so nobody reads a max as a quantile.

Influence and ownership. I owned the question, the comparison and the plan statistic; the data team built the distributions; planners owned the parameters and the buffers; Finance and planning signed the policy that used it. The conflict was commercial: quoting 34 days where the brochure said 18 — answered with the number that decided it: a fast promise missed 60% of the time costs expedites and trust; the honest date costs only optics, and where speed truly mattered we fixed the lane, not the number.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • % — shipments late against the plan on the example lane 60% (brochure) → 1% (ranked p99); program level: lead-time accuracy 99% on 200 lanes at the p99 promise (record).
  • Time — planned lead time on the example lane 18 → 34 days: the plan now says what 99 of 100 shipments do.
  • # — 200 lanes measured by site, lane and mode; 580K units / ~$1.5M released later under the signed rule (record, one-time — S8).
  • $ — on the example lane only: ≈$9.0K a year modeled (carry on the 3,500-unit pad + expedites on late arrivals) — an illustrative lane, never scaled by 200 and never added to S8’s ≈$0.6M or S6’s $2M.
  • Quality — every plan statistic reported with n and an interval; the max of a thin lane is never presented as its p99.

Scope & influencePlanners, logistics, commercial, Finance and a data team that did not report to me; the commercial pushback on 34 vs 18 resolved by the 60%-late number.

The five facets NEW — data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions; each labeled so the interviewer can see all five are present
1 · Data analysisData: shipment × lane (site × ship-from × mode), promised date (PO-line schedule) vs actual receipt, 12+ months; joins PO line → goods receipt → lane key; cuts by lane, mode, site, quarter. Revealed: bias +3 d (t = 15), 60% of shipments slower than plan, ERP σ 8 vs measured 4, normal-fit p99 holds 96.5%.
2 · Solution designOptions: plan to the mean · brochure + planner pad · normal-fit p99 · ranked (empirical) p99. Chosen: ranked p99 — distribution-free, defensible to the tail the business cares about; why: it is the only candidate whose coverage does not depend on an assumed shape.
3 · Math & data logicMath: bias = 21 − 18 = +3.0 d; SE = 4/√400 = 0.20; t = 15.0 · late share 240/400 = 60% (Wilson 95% [55.1%, 64.7%]) · normal p99 = 21 + 2.33 × 4 = 30.32, 14/400 beyond → 96.5% · ranked p99 = t(⌈0.99 × 400⌉) = t(396) = 34 d · order-statistic 95% interval t(392)…t(400).
4 · ImplementationImplementation: spec → data team → lane file → comparison → planning sign-off → plan statistic moves to the ranked p99 under the S8 release rule; drift flag + refresh cadence; thin lanes print their interval. Risk: thin lanes — handled by cohort pooling or a longer window (VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH what was actually done).
5 · Probe questionsProbe questions: n and representativeness · +3 d noise vs signal · interval on a p99 from 4 observations · seasonality · thin lanes · why rank not fit — all answered with numbers below and in the Trap Drills.
Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to a statistician

Analytics — n = 400 on one lane. Is that representative of 200 lanes?

No, and I say so. It is the busiest lane, about eight shipments a week. The master’s illustrative volume is about 10,000 shipments a year across 200 lanes — roughly 50 on an average lane — so the example shows the mechanism and the same three tests ran per lane. At n = 50 the order-statistic interval for the p99 is open-ended above: t(48) up to “unbounded” — the maximum is only a lower bound, which is why thin lanes need a longer window or cohort pooling. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH what the program did for thin lanes.

Analytics — Is a three-day difference statistically meaningful or just practically meaningful?

Both. Standard error of the mean is 4 ÷ √400 = 0.20 day, so the 95% interval on the actual mean is 21 ± 0.39 — the brochure sits fifteen standard errors away. Practically, 3 days × 500 units a day is 1,500 units of exposure per cycle that the plan did not know about.

Analytics — Your p99 is the 396th of 400. What is the confidence interval?

Wide, and I print it: with n = 400 the 95% interval for the true p99 runs from the 392nd-ranked shipment (between 31 and 34 days on this lane) to the slowest one (35 or more). In-sample coverage of the ranked plan is 99% by construction; the honest sampling interval on that coverage is 97.5%–99.6% — which is why I validated on a held-out quarter (S8c) and put a drift flag on every lane.

Analytics — Why not just mean + 2.33σ?

Because lead-time tails are right-skewed and the shortcut understates them. On this lane it gives 30.32 days; 14 of 400 shipments, 3.5%, ran longer, so a “99%” plan delivered 96.5%. On the whiteboard sample — 20 shipments, mean 12.5, σ 2.64 — the normal p99 is 12.5 + 2.33 × 2.64 = 18.65 against a ranked 22: about 3.4 days short, the canon figure.

Analytics — What about seasonality in the window?

The window was twelve months or more, so every quarter is inside the sample and the p99 prices the slow season rather than ignoring it. The test I would add is the quarterly cut: the +3-day bias should carry the same sign in all four quarters before I call it a parameter error rather than one bad season. The record does not carry that cut. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH

Shadow / closing — What would you do differently?

Lead with the thin lane, not the busy one — show the interval first. A p99 from fifty shipments is a maximum with a label on it, and the plan page should say so.

+- Consequence A (stock): 7-day pad x 500 units = 3,500 units held to cover the brochure
+- Consequence B (flow): late arrivals vs the planning date -> expedites
+- Fix: plan statistic = ranked p99 (distribution-free) + interval + drift flag

The math — step-by-step derivation

3Is it noise?SE = σ/√n = 4/√400 = 0.20 d; t = 3.0/0.20 = 15.0; 95% CI of mean = 21 ± 1.96 × 0.20 = [20.61, 21.39]measured σ = 4 dt = 15.0 — not noise
4Late vs brochure240 / 400 = 60.0%; Wilson 95% [55.1%, 64.7%]counts of shipments slower than 18 d60% late
5Spread errorERP σ = 8 vs measured 4 → ratio 2.0, variance ratio 4.0; CI of σ ≈ 4 ± 1.96 × 4/√(2 × 399) = [3.72, 4.28]ERP default 8 d (illustrative); large-sample approximation8 is far outside the interval
6Tail error — bell curve21 + 2.33 × 4 = 30.32 d; 14 / 400 = 3.5% beyond → coverage 96.5%z = 2.33 (99%, toolkit label)a “99%” plan that holds 96.5%
7Tail error — vs brochureranked p99 − brochure = 34 − 18 = 16 dranked p99 = t(396)16 d short on the tail
8The manual patch7 d × 500 units/day = 3,500 unitspad and demand rate illustrative3,500 units of pad
