Total impact statement — say it like this

“On one busy lane the brochure said eighteen days; the actual mean was twenty-one, a three-day gap that is fifteen standard errors wide, and sixty percent of shipments ran slower than the number in the system. The system’s default spread was eight days; measured, it was four. A bell curve would have promised a ninety-ninth percentile of thirty point three, and fourteen of four hundred shipments ran past it; ranking the shipments gives thirty-four, which held ninety-nine percent. The record is ninety-nine percent lead-time accuracy on two hundred lanes. The lane is an example and the dollars around it are my model on illustrative inputs — about nine thousand a year on that one lane, and I never multiply it by two hundred.”

If challenged — the full answers

The lane is the example, not the data. “The program-level result is the record — 200 lanes, 99%, 580K units, about $1.5M. The lane shows how I got there; its counts are the loop-packet example and I say so.” The interval is wide. “Yes. With 400 shipments the p99 rests on the top four; I print the interval and validate out of sample in S8c. With 50 shipments I would not call a maximum a p99.” The dollars are small. “One lane, modeled, on purpose — I would rather show the arithmetic than a program-wide number I can’t derive.”

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you dug into the data to find the real problem. Dive Deep

Open (first two sentences): Buffers kept growing and nobody could say why, so I stopped collecting opinions and pulled the shipment scans on our busiest lane. A year of history, about 400 shipments, planned date against actual receipt.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: the plan said 18 days on that lane and every planner padded a week by hand. Task: find how the number was wrong before anyone changed a parameter. Action: I compared plan to actual three ways — the mean was 21 days, three days late and fifteen standard errors from the brochure; the ERP’s assumed spread was 8 days against a measured 4; and the tail ran to 34 days. I showed it as the carrier’s own data on one lane. Result: planners stopped debating whose buffer was right and agreed the parameter was the problem; that became the p99 promise and the 580K-unit release.

Land this number: 60% of shipments slower than the brochure (240 of 400); mean 21 vs 18 — t = 15. · Pivot to NACF: NACF inbound runs on promised dates — ASN ETAs and appointment slots; the same plan-vs-actual by vendor lane is the first diagnostic I would run. · Do NOT say: Do not say I built the distributions — the data team did, to my requirement; do not say “400 on every lane.”

Q2 Tell me about a time the data contradicted what everyone believed. Are Right, A Lot

Open (first two sentences): The belief was that the carrier was missing its transit times. The scans said the carrier was delivering in about 21 days and we were planning on 18.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: service was shaky and the room’s theory was carrier performance — re-contract, escalate. Task: decide whether this was carrier management or planning. Action: I did not argue; I compared actual to the planned number and to the ERP’s assumed spread; the options ranked themselves — the carrier was consistent, our parameter was wrong. Result: the fix moved to planning parameters, not carrier contracts, and the lane held 99% at the ranked p99.

Land this number: Mean 21 d vs plan 18 d; p99 34 d. · Pivot to NACF: In NACF terms: before blaming a carrier or a vendor for dock chaos, check whether the inbound plan’s lead-time parameter was ever right. · Do NOT say: Do not imply the carriers were perfect — say the distribution had a tail; the point is the plan ignored it.

Q3 Tell me about a time you refused to accept a metric that looked good enough. Insist on the Highest Standards

Open (first two sentences): A bell-curve p99 looked right — 30.3 days — and it was a “99%” plan that held 96.5%. I would not ship a 99% label on a 96.5% plan.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: the standard shortcut, mean plus 2.33 standard deviations, was what the planning system and most analysts would use. Task: choose the plan statistic. Action: I scored four candidate plans on the same 400 shipments — mean 60%, brochure plus pad 93%, normal p99 96.5%, ranked p99 99% — and printed the sampling interval on the winner so nobody mistook in-sample for guaranteed. Result: the ranked p99 became the statistic; the interval is why I also ran a held-out quarter.

Land this number: 96.5% vs 99% — 14 vs 4 of 400. · Pivot to NACF: For NACF placement and WOC targets, the same discipline: name the service level, then check what the distribution actually delivers. · Do NOT say: Do not call 99% “guaranteed”; say in-sample by construction, interval 97.5–99.6.

Q4 Tell me about a time you persuaded a skeptical group with data. Earn Trust

Open (first two sentences): Commercial hated the number — 34 days where the brochure said 18. I did not defend it; I showed them what 18 had cost.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: commercial teams quote what the system says, and a longer date reads as slower. Task: get a defensible promise adopted without an organizational fight. Action: one lane, three numbers — 18 brochure, 21 mean, 34 p99 — and the price of the status quo: a fast date missed 60% of the time costs expedites and trust, the honest date costs only optics. Where speed genuinely mattered I offered to fix the lane, not the number. Result: planning signed the plan statistic and commercial stopped quoting the brochure.

Land this number: 60% late at the quoted date vs 1% at the plan. · Pivot to NACF: Amazon’s promise engine faces the same trade at scale; I would lead with the cost of the missed promise, not the length of the date. · Do NOT say: Do not say commercial was wrong to want speed — say the lever was the lane, not the label.

Same story, three angles NEW — three different openers, headline metrics and LPs from the same facts
(i) Data / analytical

LP: Dive Deep

Open: “The brochure was wrong three different ways.”

Headline metric: t = 15 on the 3-day bias; 60% of shipments slower than plan

  • Location: +3 d, SE 0.20 d, 15 standard errors.
  • Spread: ERP σ 8 vs measured 4 — wrong the other way, hence the pad.
  • Tail: normal p99 30.3 holds 96.5%; ranked p99 34 holds 99%.
  • One lane, three numbers, the carrier’s own scans.
(ii) Conflict / influence / backbone

LP: Have Backbone; Earn Trust

Open: “Commercial wanted to keep quoting 18; I would not put an 18 on a promise that failed 60% of the time.”

Headline metric: 60% late at the brochure date vs 1% at p99

  • The objection was real: 34 reads slower than 18.
  • I priced the status quo — expedites and trust — instead of arguing.
  • Offered the lane fix where speed mattered.
  • Planning signed the statistic; the brochure became a labeled reference.
(iii) Failure — learning — what I would do differently

LP: Learn and Be Curious

Open: “I led with the busiest lane because it had the data — and that was my analytical shortcut.”

Headline metric: n = 400 busy lane vs ≈ 50 on an average lane; p99 interval open-ended above at n = 50

  • Honest limit: a thin lane’s max is not its p99.
  • I would now show the interval first and pool thin lanes by cohort.
  • The held-out quarter (S8c) and the drift flag were the backstops.
  • VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH what the program actually did on thin lanes.
Trap drills NEW — the three nastiest follow-ups a statistically rigorous interviewer throws

T1 “Your p99 rests on four observations. How can that be a plan?”

The numbers: n = 400 → 95% interval t(392)…t(400); coverage interval 97.5%–99.6%; n = 50 → open-ended above; held-out quarter 99.1% (loop packet; S8c).

Say exactly: “It rests on the top four, and I print the interval — ninety-seven and a half to ninety-nine point six on coverage. That is why I validated on a held-out quarter and put a drift flag on every lane; on a thin lane I would not call a maximum a ninety-ninth percentile.”

T2 “How do you know the three-day gap is not one bad quarter?”

The numbers: SE 0.20 d; t = 15.0; window ≥ 12 months; the test I would add: same sign in all four quarterly cuts.

Say exactly: “Fifteen standard errors across a twelve-month window is not noise, but one bad quarter could still dominate it — the check is the quarterly cut, same sign in all four. The record doesn’t carry that cut and I would not claim it.”

T3 “What was the counterfactual — what would the plan have delivered if you had done nothing?”

The numbers: Brochure plan late 240/400 = 60%; brochure + pad 28/400 = 7% (93% served); ranked p99 4/400 = 1%.

Say exactly: “On the same 400 shipments the brochure plan was late sixty percent of the time and the brochure-plus-pad plan seven percent; the ranked p99 plan one percent — that is the counterfactual, shipment by shipment, with the interval.”

S8b — Elementum — safety-stock formula redesign: one formula nobody could defend plus a week of pad → two honest terms; one lane released 4,835 units, one lane took 1,383 in, 580K net NEW

LPs this story serves here: Invent and Simplify (primary — two terms replace a formula and a pad) · Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit (a rule that adds stock where the tail is fat) · Frugality (less stock, better service) · Ownership (a release rule someone owns after I leave)
Process family: Plan / Source seam — inventory-policy formula and release rule on top of the measured lane distributions (S8a → S8 → S8b)
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each NEW
  • Invent and Simplify — I replaced “z·√(LT·σ_d² + d²·σ_LT²) plus a week of pad” with two terms a planner can read — lead-time cover to the measured p99 and demand cover over the mean lead time.
  • Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit — The rule I wrote cuts both ways — on a lane whose measured tail is fatter than the assumption it adds stock — and I kept it two-sided when a one-way cut would have been easier to sell.
  • Frugality — Lane A went 12,937 → 8,102 units while late arrivals fell from 7-in-100 to 1-in-100: less stock and better service from one honest number.
  • Ownership — The release was a signed planning act with a drift flag and a standing stockout-and-expedite report planning owns — the formula does not need me to keep working.
Honesty label. I owned the formula redesign, the two worked lanes and the release rule; planners owned the parameters and Finance signed each lane. Lane A (d = 500, σ_d = 150, brochure 18 d, ERP σ_LT = 8 d, measured mean 21 / σ 4 / p99 34) and Lane B (d = 200, σ_d = 80, brochure 24, measured mean 30 / σ 11 / p99 58) are the loop packet’s worked lanes; the roll-up split — about 150 lanes releasing an average of 4,500 units, about 50 lanes adding an average of 1,900 — is the loop packet’s illustrative decomposition. The record is the net: 580K units / ~$1.5M, zero stockouts. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH — the master and this packet’s S8 say fat-tailed lanes’ buffer was “kept”; the loop packet says about 50 lanes took stock in. Say “kept, and on the worst lanes raised” only if that is what happened.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. A six-site manufacturer’s inventory buffers were set by a textbook formula fed an assumed lead-time swing of eight days that nobody had measured, plus a week of pad that every planner added by hand. Task. Redesign the buffer so it said what the lanes actually did, and write the rule for when capital could come out. Action. I took the formula apart: on the example lane, 97.5% of its variance came from that unmeasured eight-day swing — the formula was mostly measuring a guess. I replaced it with two terms: lead-time cover — units per day times the distance from the average lead time to the measured ninety-ninth percentile — and demand cover over the average lead time. The pad went to zero because the plan now held 99%. The rule cut both ways: release only where the measured spread was inside the assumption, and where the tail was fatter, keep or add. Result. Primary metric: safety stock on the example lane 12,937 → 8,102 units, 37% less, with late arrivals 7-in-100 → 1-in-100; the program released 580K units, about $1.5 million, with zero stockouts — record, one-time working capital.

20-second version NEW

I replaced a formula that was 97% an unmeasured guess, plus a hand-added pad, with two honest terms — cover to the measured p99 and demand cover — and a two-sided release rule: 580K units out net, zero stockouts.

NACF bridge — one sentence

The NACF version is the inbound booklet’s INV-B: SS = z·σ·√LT — I would show which of the two variance terms dominates before anyone moves a buffer, and I would write the release rule so it can raise a buffer as well as cut it.

Situation / Task — the problem space

S — The same six-site manufacturer. Safety stock was the textbook form, z × √(LT·σ_d² + d²·σ_LT²), with the brochure lead time and a σ_LT of 8 days that was the ERP’s default, plus an informal manual pad of about a week per lane. Nobody could say which part of the buffer was protecting against demand and which against transit — and on the loud lanes the answer was always “add more.”

T — Own the formula and the release rule: say what each unit of buffer is for, size it from the measured distribution (S8a), and release capital only where the measured evidence supported it — with the exception written into the rule before anyone asked.

Baselines on the table: Lane A buffer 12,937 units (25.9 days of demand) · pad 3,500 units uncounted in the ERP · 7-in-100 shipments late against the plan in force · no statement of what service level the buffer bought.

Action — five gated phases
  • Discovery — took the ERP formula apart on one lane: the demand part contributed 405,000 to the variance, the lead-time part 16,000,000 — 97.5% of it an assumed number. Exit bar: planning and Finance agree the buffer is a parameter question, not a service-level argument.
  • Plan & Design — two terms — lead-time cover d × (p99 − mean) using the ranked p99 from S8a, and demand cover z × σ_d × √(mean lead time) — pad to zero; release rule: release only where the measured spread and tail are inside the assumption and lane accuracy holds; where they are not, the buffer stays or rises. Exit bar: Planning and Finance sign the rule’s text, including the add-stock clause.
  • Build & Test — the data team computed every lane; I recomputed two lanes by hand and ran the normal-formula counterfactual beside them. Exit bar: Lane A and Lane B reproduce to the unit, and the counterfactual is on the page.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — released lane by lane under the signed rule with Finance signing each lane; stockout-and-expedite watch from day one. Exit bar: no lane released without its lane accuracy and signature. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH wave structure and lane counts per wave are not in the record.
  • Hypercare / Close — drift flag per lane — p99 recomputed on refresh, buffer decision re-opens if accuracy falls — and the watch report handed to planning. Exit bar: planning re-applies the rule on its own refresh without me.

Influence and ownership. I owned the formula, the rule and the two worked lanes; the data team built the distributions; Finance signed each lane; planners owned the parameters. The temptation was a blanket cut because a one-way release is the easiest thing to sell to Finance — Lane B is the page that shows why not: a blanket cut of the same size would leave about half of what the measured tail needs.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — 580K units / ~$1.5M safety stock released, net (record, one-time working capital; ≈$2.59/unit derived); ≈$0.3M/yr carry on it at 20% (modeled) — inside S8’s ≈$0.6M, never added to S6’s $2M.
  • # — Lane A 12,937 → 8,102 units (−4,835); Lane B 5,238 → 6,621 (+1,383); roll-up 150 × 4,500 − 50 × 1,900 = 580,000 (loop-packet decomposition, illustrative).
  • % — late arrivals 7-in-100 → 1-in-100 on Lane A (loop-packet example lane); lane accuracy 99% on 200 lanes (record).
  • Quality — zero stockouts in the watch window (record); the rule is two-sided and written; the pad is gone.
  • Customer — the production line did not stop on a released lane; the planners’ pad stopped being a second, hidden policy.

The five facets NEW — data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions; each labeled so the interviewer can see all five are present
1 · Data analysisData: lane × (d, σ_d, brochure LT, measured LT mean / σ / ranked p99) — Lane A and B; variance decomposition of the ERP formula: 97.5% lead-time part. Revealed: a guessed σ_LT dominated the buffer.
2 · Solution designOptions: keep the ERP form with a measured σ_LT · normal-fit p99 · ranked-p99 cover + demand cover. Chosen: two terms on the ranked p99 — it does not assume the lead-time tail is a bell curve. Release rule: two-sided, signed.
3 · Math & data logicMath: 9,437 + 3,500 pad = 12,937; after = 500 × (34 − 21) + 2.33 × 150 × √21 = 6,500 + 1,602 = 8,102; Lane B +1,383; z-table for the demand term; sensitivity to the assumed σ_LT.
4 · ImplementationImplementation: data team per lane → hand-check two lanes → signed rule → lane-by-lane release with Finance signatures → watch report and drift flag handed to planning.
5 · Probe questionsProbe questions: independence and normality of the demand term · cycle-service vs fill-rate · why z = 2.33 · blanket-cut counterfactual · what happens on a thin lane — see Trap Drills.
Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to a statistician

Analytics — Your formula adds a demand term and a lead-time term. Are they independent?

The ERP form assumes demand per day and lead time are independent; I kept that for the demand term only, and it is 19.8% of the after-buffer (1,602 of 8,102). The lead-time term — 80.2% — is empirical, not a bell curve. A fifty-percent error in the demand term moves the total by about ±10%. If demand surges when transit is slow the two correlate and the true figure is higher; I would test that on the lane history before calling it closed.

Analytics — Why z = 2.33?

Because the signed service level was 99% and Φ(2.33) = 0.9901. The price of more is steep and nonlinear: the demand cover on Lane A is 1,131 at 95%, 1,602 at 99% and 2,124 at 99.9% — +522 units (32.6%) for the last nine-tenths of a point.

Analytics — Is 99% a fill rate or a cycle service level?

A cycle service level — the probability a replenishment cycle ends without a stockout, measured as lane accuracy against the p99 promise. Fill rate — the share of units shipped from stock — is a different, much higher number on the same buffer; I do not mix the two and I say which one I mean.

Analytics — Why release 4,835 units and not 8,009, which is what the textbook form with the measured σ would release?

Because the textbook form with the measured σ_LT = 4 gives 4,928 units after, i.e. a release of 8,009, and it plans to 30.3 days when 3.5% of shipments run past that. The extra 3,174 units is exactly the tail the bell curve cannot see — that is the S8a result paid for in units.

Shadow / closing — What would you do differently?

Put Lane B on the first page. The two-sided result — some lanes need more stock — was the part that earned Finance’s trust, and it should have been the opening exhibit, not the second slide.

9Blanket-cut counterfactual on BLane A’s cut 37.4% applied to B: 5,238 × (1 − 0.3737) ≈ 3,280; ÷ 6,621 = 49.5%illustrative counterfactual (a blanket rule)leaves 49.5% of the measured need — short 3,341 units ≈ 16.7 days of demand

Solution flow — formula, rule, watch

 ERP: PO lines + goods receipts --> S8a lane distribution (n, mean, sigma, ranked p99)
                                      |
                                      v
 per lane:  LT cover = d x (p99 - mean_LT)     demand cover = z x sigma_d x sqrt(mean_LT)
                         \                       /
                          v                     v
                     buffer = LT cover + demand cover          (pad = 0)
                                      |
        measured spread/tail inside assumption AND lane accuracy 99%?
           yes -> release (Finance signs the lane)      no -> keep or raise buffer
                                      |
             refresh: p99 recomputed; accuracy falls -> decision re-opens (drift flag)

Total impact statement — say it like this

“The old buffer was a textbook formula fed an eight-day lead-time swing nobody had measured — ninety-seven percent of its variance was that guess — plus a week of hand-added pad. I replaced it with two terms a planner can read: cover to the measured ninety-ninth percentile, and demand cover. On the example lane that took twelve thousand nine hundred units to about eight thousand one hundred with late arrivals falling from seven in a hundred to one; on a lane with a fatter tail it added about fourteen hundred units, because the rule cuts both ways. The program record is five hundred eighty thousand units, about one and a half million dollars, released net with zero stockouts — the lane arithmetic is the loop packet’s example and I label it.”

If challenged — the full answers

The split is illustrative. “The 150-and-50 decomposition is how I explain the net; the record is 580K units, about $1.5M, zero stockouts.” The fat-tail lanes — kept or raised? “The rule keeps or raises where the measured tail is fatter than the assumption; whether a given lane saw stock go in is a lane-level fact I would pull from the release log rather than assert.” The normal form is simpler. “And it plans to 30.3 days where 3.5% of shipments run past — I would rather defend 4,835 units than 8,009.”

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you simplified something complicated. Invent and Simplify

Open (first two sentences): Safety stock on our lanes was a formula nobody could defend plus a week of pad nobody could count. I replaced both with two terms a planner could read.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: the ERP buffer was z times a root of two variances, with an eight-day lead-time swing nobody had measured — 98% of the formula was that guess — and planners added a week on top. Task: say what each unit of buffer was for. Action: I split it into lead-time cover to the measured p99 and demand cover over the mean lead time, set the pad to zero, and wrote the release rule. Result: Lane A went from 12,937 to 8,102 units with late arrivals falling from seven in a hundred to one, and the program released 580K units with zero stockouts.

Land this number: 12,937 → 8,102 units; 97.5% of the old variance was one unmeasured input. · Pivot to NACF: INV-B is the same two-variance structure; I would show a planner which term dominates before touching a number. · Do NOT say: Do not say I replaced the ERP; say I changed two parameters and the rule that governs them.

Q2 Tell me about a time you held your position when it was the harder path. Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit

Open (first two sentences): The easy rule to sell was a release-only rule. I wrote one that could also add stock — and defended it.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: the obvious design was to cut buffers and report the release. Task: write a rule that would survive an audit. Action: I wrote it two-sided — release where the measured spread was inside the assumption, keep or add where it was not — and showed the alternative on Lane B: a blanket cut of Lane A’s size would leave 50% of what the measured tail needs. Finance and Planning signed it with the add-stock clause. Result: 580K net, zero stockouts, and the credibility to do the next release. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH whether a specific person asked for a one-way cut.

Land this number: Lane B +1,383 units; blanket cut leaves 49.5% of need. · Pivot to NACF: In placement, the same discipline: a rule that can only move one way is a target, not a policy. · Do NOT say: Do not name or imply a person pushed back unless it is true; say “the easy design was.”

Q3 Tell me about a time you did more with less. Frugality

Open (first two sentences): Less stock and better service — on one lane, 37% fewer units and seven late shipments in a hundred became one.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: working capital sat in buffers that were partly folklore. Task: take capital out without moving the service line. Action: replaced the pad with a measured p99 plan and kept the buffer wherever the tail demanded it. Result: Lane A 4,835 units out (≈$12,523) with fewer late arrivals; program 580K units, about $1.5M, zero stockouts.

Land this number: 580K units / ~$1.5M net, zero stockouts. · Pivot to NACF: WOC targets are the NACF equivalent — fewer weeks of cover only where the measured distribution supports it. · Do NOT say: Do not claim recurring savings as the headline; the $1.5M is one-time working capital.

Q4 Tell me about something you owned beyond the delivery date. Ownership

Open (first two sentences): A release is not done when the units leave the shelf. It is done when the rule runs without you.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a buffer cut that nobody re-checks decays into a new brochure. Task: make the release self-correcting. Action: drift flag per lane — p99 recomputed on refresh, the buffer decision re-opens if accuracy falls — and the stockout-and-expedite watch handed to planning as a standing report. Result: planning re-applies the rule on its own refresh; the watch showed zero stockouts.

Land this number: Zero stockouts in the watch window (record). · Pivot to NACF: NACF inbound needs the same drift flag on every lead-time and WOC parameter. · Do NOT say: Do not say I ran the watch; say planning owns it.

Headline metric: normal 30.3 d vs ranked 34 d; 8,009 vs 4,835 release

Trap drills NEW — the three nastiest follow-ups a statistically rigorous interviewer throws

T1 “Your safety-stock formula assumes normality and independence. Why trust it?”

The numbers: Demand term = 1,602 of 8,102 = 19.8%; ±50% error → ±10% of the total; lead-time term empirical (ranked p99).

Say exactly: “Only the smaller term assumes a bell curve — about a fifth of the buffer; the lead-time term, four-fifths, is the measured ranked p99 with no distribution assumed, and a fifty-percent error in the demand term moves the total by about ten percent.”

T2 “Is 99% a fill rate or a cycle service level, and what does 99.9% cost?”

The numbers: Demand cover at 99% = 1,602; at 99.9% = 2,124 (+522, +32.6%); z 2.33 → 3.09.

Say exactly: “Ninety-nine percent is a cycle service level — probability of no stockout per replenishment — not a fill rate; going to 99.9 adds about five hundred units on the demand term alone, a third more, and I would only buy it where a stockout costs more than that.”

T3 “Why not just cut everything by the same percentage — it is simpler and Finance would like it?”

The numbers: Lane A cut 37.4%; applied to Lane B: 3,280 vs need 6,621 → 49.5%; short 3,341 units = 16.7 days of demand.

Say exactly: “A blanket cut of Lane A’s size would leave Lane B with half the buffer its measured tail needs — about seventeen days of demand uncovered — which is a line-down waiting to happen; the two-sided rule is what let Finance sign the release.”

S8c — Elementum — validation and back-test design: how I proved the p99 plan held out of sample and was not a quiet quarter — held-out quarter 99.1%, zero stockouts, and what that can and cannot show NEW

LPs this story serves here: Are Right, A Lot (primary — test my own decision out of time) · Insist on the Highest Standards (per-lane gate, no pooling) · Earn Trust (the limits of the test on the page) · Learn and Be Curious (shadow-mode the drift flags first)
Process family: Plan / Source seam — validation and monitoring design for a parameter change before capital moves
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each NEW
  • Are Right, A Lot — I did not trust in-sample 99%: I trained on twelve months, scored a later quarter the plan had never seen, and read the result against an interval — 99.1% [98.7%, 99.4%].
  • Insist on the Highest Standards — The bar was per lane, 99%, before any buffer released — no pooling to hide a bad lane inside a good average.
  • Earn Trust — I put what the test cannot show on the page — a lane has about thirteen shipments a quarter — so Finance signed knowing the limits.
  • Learn and Be Curious — My own lesson is shadow-mode: run the drift flags against history before they go live.
Honesty label. Record: validated on a held-out quarter at 99.1% (loop packet, secondary source); zero stockouts in the watch window; Finance signed each lane; drift flag per lane. The held-out sample size (2,500 shipments = the master’s illustrative ~10,000 a year ÷ 4), the old-plan late share (7%, the loop-packet example lane applied program-wide), the 150 releasing lanes and the 13-week window are illustrative inputs for the arithmetic — not the record. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH (a) the length of the watch window, (b) which quarter was held out and whether it was a peak or a quiet quarter, (c) whether any contemporaneous control was run, (d) whether one held-out quarter or several were used.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. We had replaced brochure lead times with measured p99 plans and were about to take capital out of 150-odd lanes. A plan fitted to a year of history will look perfect on that year — that is not evidence. Task. Prove the plan would hold on shipments it had never seen, and that the result was not a quiet season. Action. I trained each lane on twelve months and scored the next quarter out of time. At program level that is about 2,500 shipments; 22 ran past the plan — 99.1% held, with a 95% interval of 98.7% to 99.4%, clearly better than the 96.5% a bell-curve plan delivers, and statistically indistinguishable from the 99% we promised. I scored the same quarter’s shipments against the old brochure-plus-pad plan, so the season hits both plans equally. And I was explicit about the limit: on one lane a quarter is about thirteen shipments, so lane-level validation is the drift flag, not a quarterly test. Result. Primary metric: 99.1% on the held-out quarter against the 99% promise, then zero stockouts across the released lanes — record — with 580K units, about $1.5 million, released net.

20-second version NEW

I back-tested the p99 plan on a quarter it had never seen — 99.1%, interval 98.7 to 99.4 — scored the same shipments against the old plan so a quiet season could not flatter it, and kept a per-lane drift flag because a lane alone has too few shipments to validate.

NACF bridge — one sentence

For NACF placement and WOC targets I would want the same out-of-time test before any buffer comes out: train on the past, score a later quarter, and read the result as an interval, not a number.

Situation / Task — the problem space

S — Elementum, six-site manufacturer. The measured p99 plans (S8a) and the formula (S8b) were ready; releasing 580K units of buffer was irreversible in practice, and the one thing a plan fitted to history will always do is look good on that history. The audience — Finance and Planning — would sign lane by lane only on evidence they could challenge.

T — Design the validation: an out-of-time test that could fail, an interval around the answer, a defense against “it was a quiet quarter,” a live watch after release, and a drift flag that outlives me.

Baselines on the table: in-sample coverage 99% by construction (396 of 400 on the example lane) · no out-of-sample measurement existed · brochure-plus-pad plan late 7 in 100 on the example lane · no stockout metric tied to the release.

Action — five gated phases
  • Discovery — named the failure mode — in-sample coverage is 99% by construction — and the second one, seasonality: a quarter that happens to be easy flatters any plan. Exit bar: Finance and Planning agree what result would make us stop.
  • Plan & Design — train on twelve months so every season is inside the plan; hold out the next quarter; metric = arrival vs plan; score the same shipments against the old plan; pre-register the pass bar (99% promise, read as an interval) and the per-lane gate (99%, no pooling) before looking. Exit bar: bar and metric signed before the data is scored.
  • Build & Test — scored the held-out quarter: 22 of 2,500 beyond plan, 99.1%; the paired score against the old plan on the same shipments; the power calculation. Exit bar: result reproduced by the data team; interval and power on the page.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — release lane by lane under the signed rule; live watch — stockouts and expedites by lane — from day one; zero stockouts in the watch window. Exit bar: no lane released without its own accuracy ≥ 99%. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH the watch window length.
  • Hypercare / Close — drift flag per lane (p99 recomputed on refresh; decision re-opens if accuracy falls) and a standing report owned by planning; lesson recorded: shadow-mode the flags against history before they go live. Exit bar: planning re-runs the check on its own refresh.

Influence and ownership. I owned the validation design and the pass bars; the data team scored the lanes; Finance and Planning signed. The conflict was pace: releasing on the in-sample result would have been faster and indistinguishable on a slide — I held the release to the out-of-time test and wrote the per-lane gate so a good average could not hide a bad lane.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • % — held-out quarter 99.1% against a 99% promise (loop packet; interval 98.7%–99.4% on an illustrative 2,500 shipments); lane accuracy 99% on 200 lanes (record).
  • # — zero stockouts in the watch window (record); with 13 weeks × 150 releasing lanes = 1,950 lane-weeks that bounds the stockout rate below 0.15% per lane-week at 95% confidence (rule of three; window illustrative).
  • $ — the validation supports the 580K units / ~$1.5M release (record, one-time); it is not a separate saving. Cost of trusting the bell curve instead: ≈$79K/yr of expedites on the example inputs (modeled, a counterfactual, not a claim).
  • Quality — a pre-registered pass bar, an interval, a paired replay, and a drift flag.
  • Customer — no line stopped on a released lane.

ImpactThe validation is what let 580K units / ~$1.5M come out with zero stockouts.

The five facets NEW — data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions; each labeled so the interviewer can see all five are present
1 · Data analysisData: shipments per lane, 12 months training + the next quarter held out; arrival vs plan; same shipments scored against the old plan. Revealed: 22/2,500 beyond plan, 99.1%, interval 98.7–99.4%; the old plan late ≈ 7%.
2 · Solution designOptions: in-sample fit · hold-out quarter · rolling-origin back-test · live shadow. Chosen: out-of-time hold-out plus paired replay plus live watch plus per-lane drift flag; rolling-origin is the upgrade.
3 · Math & data logicMath: Wilson interval · z vs 96.5% = 7.13, vs 99% = 0.60 · power 99% at a true 98.0% · expected out-of-sample coverage of the ranked p99 = r/(n+1) = 98.75% ± 0.55 · rule of three on zero stockouts.
4 · ImplementationImplementation: pre-registered bars → score → paired replay → lane-by-lane signature → watch → drift flag → standing report.
5 · Probe questionsProbe questions: interval and power · seasonality · per-lane n · what zero stockouts proves · what a thin lane does — see Trap Drills.
Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to a statistician

Analytics — A ranked p99 from 400 shipments, tested on new data. What coverage should you expect?

Not 99%. The r-th of n ranked observations covers, on average, r/(n+1) of the future distribution: 396/401 = 98.75% with a standard deviation of 0.55 points. So 99.1% is well inside what the method predicts — a 29% chance of a coverage that high or higher. On a thin lane the maximum of 50 shipments covers 50/51 = 98.04% ± 1.92 points and has a 7.7% chance of covering less than 95%.

Analytics — Is 99.1% different from 99%?

No. 22 late of 2,500; standard error under 99% is 0.20 points, z = 0.60. It is different from the bell-curve plan’s 96.5% (z = 7.13). The test is built to detect a shortfall, not a surplus: it rejects 99% only if coverage falls below 98.67%.

Analytics — How much power did the test have?

At 2,500 shipments: 99% power against a true coverage of 98.0%, 76% against 98.5%; the smallest shortfall it detects with 80% power is about 0.53 points (98.47% true coverage). So it is good at catching a plan that is clearly wrong and blind to a plan that is half a point short — which is why the drift flag exists.

Analytics — How do you know it was not seasonality?

Three defenses and one limit. The training window held every season; the metric is arrival against plan, not volume; and I scored the same held-out shipments against the old plan, so a quiet quarter flatters both plans equally — on the illustrative program numbers the old plan was late on 175 shipments and the new on 22. The limit: one held-out quarter is one draw; the upgrade is a rolling-origin back-test with a peak quarter in it. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH which quarter was held out.

Analytics — Can you validate each lane the same way?

No. An average lane has about 50 shipments a year, roughly 13 a quarter; a lane whose true coverage is exactly 99% shows zero late shipments 87.8% of the time and one-or-more 12.2% of the time, so one late shipment in a quarter tells me nothing. Per-lane protection is the drift flag on rolling history; the quarterly test is pooled.

Shadow / closing — What would you do differently?

Shadow-mode the drift flags against history before they go live — replay a past year and count the false alarms — and start the rolling-origin back-test on day one so the peak quarter is in it.

Release 580K units on a p99 plan fitted to history

+- Risk 3: a lane has ~13 shipments/quarter -> pooled test + per-lane drift flag
+- Risk 4: zero stockouts proves little in a short window -> rule-of-three bound
+- Payoff: Finance signs lane by lane; capital leaves; watch confirms

Total impact statement — say it like this

“A plan fitted to a year of history looks perfect on that year, so I held out a quarter the plan had never seen: ninety-nine point one percent held, with an interval of about ninety-eight point seven to ninety-nine point four, against ninety-six and a half for a bell-curve plan. Same shipments scored against the old plan, so a quiet season could not flatter it. After release, zero stockouts across the released lanes — which on a thirteen-week window of about two thousand lane-weeks bounds the rate under about a sixth of a percent. What the test cannot show is any single lane — a lane has about thirteen shipments a quarter — so each lane carries a drift flag. The record is five hundred eighty thousand units, about one and a half million dollars, released with zero stockouts; the interval, power and paired replay are my design arithmetic on illustrative volumes.”

If challenged — the full answers

One quarter is one draw. “Agreed — I would have preferred a rolling origin with a peak quarter, and I say so; the paired score and the live watch are the other two legs.” Zero stockouts proves little. “It bounds the rate: zero in about two thousand lane-weeks is under 0.15% per lane-week at 95% — not zero risk, a number.” The numbers are illustrative. “The 99.1% and the zero stockouts are the record; the volumes behind the intervals are my illustrative inputs and I mark them.”

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you tested your own decision and it could have failed. Are Right, A Lot

Open (first two sentences): I was about to release 580K units on a plan fitted to history, and a plan fitted to history always looks perfect on that history. So I held a quarter out.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: p99 plans per lane, buffer about to come out. Task: prove the plan held on shipments it had never seen. Action: trained on twelve months, scored the next quarter — 22 of 2,500 beyond plan, 99.1%, interval 98.7 to 99.4 — and scored the same shipments against the old plan so a quiet season could not flatter it. I wrote the pass bar before scoring. Result: Finance signed lane by lane; zero stockouts after release.

Land this number: 99.1% out of time vs 96.5% for a bell-curve plan. · Pivot to NACF: Before any NACF placement or WOC target change, the same out-of-time test on a held-back quarter. · Do NOT say: Do not say “proved”; say “held out of time, with an interval.”

Q2 Tell me about a time you set a higher bar than you were asked to. Insist on the Highest Standards

Open (first two sentences): The bar was 99% per lane before any buffer released — not 99% on average. Below it, no release, written into the act before anyone asked.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a pooled 99% can hide a bad lane inside a good average. Task: a release rule that could not be gamed by pooling. Action: per-lane accuracy bar, signed in advance; lanes under it did not release; the drift flag re-opens the decision if accuracy falls. Result: 580K units out net, zero stockouts, and no lane released on an average.

Land this number: 99% per lane across 200 lanes; 580K units / ~$1.5M. · Pivot to NACF: A placement model can pass on region average and fail one FC-region pair — I gate per cell. · Do NOT say: Do not say the bar was Finance’s; it was written into the act as my design.

Q3 Tell me about a time you had to earn trust from a skeptical partner. Earn Trust

Open (first two sentences): Finance signed lane by lane because I put what the test could not show on the same page as what it could.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: Finance was being asked to sign a release on a statistical argument. Task: make the argument checkable. Action: the interval beside the number, the power beside the interval, and the limit beside the power — an average lane has about thirteen shipments a quarter, so per-lane validation is the drift flag, not the test. Result: Finance signed each lane and the watch confirmed zero stockouts.

Land this number: 99.1% [98.7, 99.4]; per-lane n ≈ 13. · Pivot to NACF: Amazon’s bar-raising partners trust the analyst who names the limit first. · Do NOT say: Do not oversell the 2,500; it is an illustrative program-level n.

Q4 Tell me about something you learned that changed how you work. Learn and Be Curious

Open (first two sentences): What I learned is to shadow-mode the drift flags against history before they go live.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: the drift flag — rolling p99 above plan by 2 days, σ above 1.25 times the base, or four late per hundred — looked right on a whiteboard. Task: know its false-alarm rate before planners had to answer it. Action (the lesson): replay a past year and count how often the flag would have fired. Result: the flag goes live with a known alert rate; the same replay is how I would tune any threshold now.

Land this number: Rule of three: 0 in 1,950 lane-weeks → < 0.154% per lane-week. · Pivot to NACF: In NACF, a new inbound alert would be replayed against a prior peak before it pages anybody. · Do NOT say: Do not say the replay was done unless it was — say “what I would now do first.” VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH

Same story, three angles NEW — three different openers, headline metrics and LPs from the same facts
(i) Data / analytical

LP: Are Right, A Lot

Open: “A plan fitted to a year of history looks perfect on that year.”

Headline metric: 99.1% out of time, [98.7, 99.4]; expected 98.75% ± 0.55

  • Hold-out quarter, never seen.
  • z vs 96.5% = 7.13; vs 99% = 0.60.
  • Power 99% at a true 98.0%.
  • Paired replay on the same shipments.
(ii) Conflict / influence / backbone

LP: Insist on the Highest Standards

Open: “I would not release on a pooled average.”

Headline metric: 99% per lane, no pooling

  • The bar was signed before scoring.
  • Lanes under the bar did not release.
  • The drift flag re-opens the decision.
  • VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH any pressure to release early.
(iii) Failure — learning — what I would do differently

LP: Learn and Be Curious

Open: “My test could not tell me anything about a single lane.”

Headline metric: per-lane quarterly n ≈ 13; P(0 late | 99%) = 87.8%

  • One quarter is one draw — rolling origin is the upgrade.
  • Zero stockouts bounds the rate; it does not prove zero.
  • Shadow-mode the drift flags before go-live.
  • VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH whether a peak quarter was ever held out.
Trap drills NEW — the three nastiest follow-ups a statistically rigorous interviewer throws

T1 “99.1% — is that different from 99%? What was your power?”

The numbers: n = 2,500, 22 late; Wilson [98.67%, 99.42%]; z vs 99% = 0.60; power 99% at 98.0%, 76% at 98.5%; blind below ≈ 0.5 pt.

Say exactly: “It is not different from 99 — the interval is 98.7 to 99.4, z is 0.6 — and it is clearly different from the bell-curve plan at 96.5. The test catches a coverage of 98 percent about 99 times in a hundred and a half-point shortfall about three in four, so I do not read it as precise; that is why the drift flag exists.”

T2 “How do you know it was not just an easy quarter?”

The numbers: Same 2,500 shipments: old plan late 175 (7%), new plan 22; McNemar χ² = 145.4; training window 12 months, all seasons.

Say exactly: “I scored the same shipments against the old plan, so an easy quarter flatters both plans equally — the old plan was late on about a hundred and seventy-five, the new on twenty-two. One quarter is still one draw, and I would add a rolling origin with a peak quarter in it.”

T3 “Zero stockouts — what does that prove?”

The numbers: 13 weeks × 150 lanes = 1,950 lane-weeks; upper bound 3 ÷ 1,950 = 0.154% per lane-week (95%); per-lane quarter n ≈ 13 → P(0 late | 99%) = 87.8%.

Say exactly: “It bounds the rate, it does not prove zero: no events in about two thousand lane-weeks puts the stockout rate under a sixth of a percent per lane-week at ninety-five percent confidence. And it says nothing about any one lane — at thirteen shipments a quarter a lane at exactly ninety-nine percent shows no late shipment 88 percent of the time.”

S9 — Elementum — 1,000 routes asked, 597 built first: spend Pareto, a ≥95% valid-ASN gate held twice, completeness 60% → 95%, freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months NEW

LPs this story serves here: Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit (primary — re-scoped an executive’s 1,000-in-two-months ask) · Insist on the Highest Standards (held a cohort twice) · Dive Deep (Pareto, lane scoring, EDI survey) · Invent and Simplify (the standard was the data, not the transport)
Process family: Source / Deliver seam — inbound visibility onboarding at scale (lane scoring → standard → gate → scorecard)
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each NEW
  • Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit — I took the 1,000-lanes-in-two-months ask two levels up with the spend curve and replaced it with 597 lanes = 80% of spend first — then committed fully.
  • Insist on the Highest Standards — One cohort failed the ≥95% valid-ASN gate twice on a timestamp format; I held it both times — automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers.
  • Dive Deep — I built the spend Pareto and lane scoring (spend × criticality × mode × volume) and the carrier EDI-capability survey before taking a scope to the executive.
  • Invent and Simplify — The onboarding standard was the data set plus an XML/portal floor — “the standard was the data, not the transport” — and a parameterized harness replaced bespoke per-carrier maps.
Honesty label. I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor; engineers built the maps and harness to the standard; Transportation owned the carrier relationships.
60-second pitch

"Situation. The Global Head of Supply Chain wanted inbound visibility on 1,000 lanes in two months; shipment-data completeness was 60% and high-value lanes were blind. Task. I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor. Action. Discovery: spend Pareto — 597 lanes = 80% of freight spend — pre-validated with Transportation and Procurement. Plan & Design: X12 850/855/856/940/945/214/997 with an XML/portal floor; cohort gate ≥95% valid ASN. Build & Test: mapping-and-test harness; scorecards per lane. Cutover: 597 live in month one; one cohort failed twice on a timestamp format — I held it. Hypercare: completeness scorecards to 95%; ~200 next wave behind the same gate. Lesson: automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — the gate is the product. Result. Primary metric: inbound shipment-data completeness — 60% → 95%. $ impact: freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record); my model splits it into routing-guide compliance, mode discipline and expedites avoided, and adds ≈$0.4M/yr of modeled document-handling labor on top; 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one; adoption 8×."

20-second version NEW

A 1,000-lanes-in-two-months ask became 597 lanes carrying 80% of spend first behind a ≥95% valid-ASN gate I held twice: completeness 60% → 95%, freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record, not annualized), adoption 8×.

NACF bridge — one sentence

Inbound ASN completeness and validity are the first dependency of any NACF placement or WOC model — I would score vendors by volume Pareto and gate activation on valid-ASN rate, not on a date.

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The customer's Global Head of Supply Chain wanted end-to-end inbound visibility on 1,000 global lanes in two months — scope, schedule and quality all fixed. Lanes differed by orders of magnitude in spend, volume and criticality; carriers differed in EDI capability; completeness was 60% and high-value lanes were effectively blind.

T — Own the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor.

The goal: visibility that was actually trustworthy, at the spend that mattered first. The risk of taking the ask literally: automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — 1,000 lanes at 60% completeness in two months would have shipped exactly that — and without a floor for carriers without EDI, the long tail gets priced out of the standard.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: shipment-data completeness — 60%; high-value lane visibility — effectively blind, target 100% on the lanes carrying the spend; the activation standard — none existed, target a ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate a lane had to clear before going live.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  • Discovery — built the spend Pareto and lane scoring model (spend × criticality × mode × volume) and the carrier EDI-capability survey; pre-validated the 597 / ~200 / remainder scope with Transportation and Procurement before taking it up. Exit bar: lane owners cannot contradict the cut; the executive accepts the phased plan in spend share, not lane count.
  • Plan & Design — wrote the onboarding standard (document set + XML/portal floor — "the standard was the data, not the transport"), the ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, the per-lane scorecard spec (events received / expected; 997 rate; tracking-number joinability), the harness design. Exit bar: standard signed by Transportation; gate published to carriers before cohort 1; harness templates exist.
  • Build & Test — held engineers to the harness (per-partner mapping templates, test scripts, DQ checklist), test files per carrier, a parallel run per lane, scorecards live. Exit bar: each cohort's lanes clear ≥95% valid ASN in test before activation.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (per cohort) — activated cohorts only off the published gate; published the scorecards; recorded the hold decision when a cohort failed. Exit bar: cohort active only after the gate; a failing cohort held — twice, on a timestamp format.
  • Hypercare / Close — weekly completeness scorecards; harness reused for later waves; method written up as the sourcing SOP (spend file in → phased plan out). Exit bar: completeness 60% → 95%; 100% visibility on high-value lanes; remaining waves behind the same gate.

The scope analysis and the standard were mine end to end — the spend Pareto and lane scoring model, the carrier EDI-capability survey, the onboarding standard, the gate, the scorecard spec and the hold decisions; engineers built the mapping templates, test scripts and harness, and Transportation held the carrier relationships and accepted the holds. The contribution: pre-validating the 597 cut with Transportation and Procurement so no lane owner could contradict it in the leadership meeting, and publishing the ≥95% gate to carriers before cohort one — a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion.

The obstacles: carriers unable to produce valid ASNs — answered with the XML/portal floor (“the standard was the data, not the transport”) and a hold rule; a cohort that failed the gate twice on a timestamp format — held both times, fixed, then activated; bespoke maps per partner — avoided by the parameterized harness. Priorities ran on spend share, not lane count — 597 lanes carrying 80% of spend first, ~200 next, the remainder after, each behind the same gate; buy-in came from the pre-validation, then the executive accepted the phased plan off the curve. The decision challenged was the 1,000-in-two-months ask itself — taken two levels up with the spend curve, deferring ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend, priced — and I refused activating any cohort that failed the gate.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: completeness 60% → 95%; 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one; freight −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record); adoption 8×.

  • $ — Freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months on the onboarded lanes (record); my three-way split (routing-guide compliance, mode discipline, expedites and detention) decomposes the record, never adds to it; ≈$0.4M/yr modeled document-handling labor on top.
  • # — 597 of 1,000 lanes = 80% of freight spend live in month one; ~200 the next wave; the remainder behind the same gate (record).
  • % — Completeness 60% → 95%; the ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate — held twice on a cohort that failed on a timestamp format (record).
  • Time — 597 lanes live in month one; the record $2M landed within six months (record).
  • Customer — 100% visibility on high-value lanes; small carriers kept inside the standard by the XML/portal floor instead of being priced out (record).
  • Quality — The gate is the product — automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers; adoption 8×; the method written up as the sourcing SOP (record).

Against the goal stated above: completeness 60 → 95%; 100% visibility on high-value lanes; 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one at ≥95% valid ASN, the remaining waves behind the same gate — with freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months funding the next wave. The trade was explicit and priced: ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend deferred so the 597 could go live at the highest data quality — and quality was never traded: a lane that slipped below its scorecard went back to “fix,” not “live.”

2 · Solution designOptions: 1,000 lanes in two months · 597 first behind a gate · everything via EDI only. Chosen: Pareto-first, XML/portal floor, ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, scorecard per lane.
3 · Math & data logicMath: S(597) = 80% (record); completeness 480/800 = 60%; implied spend base $2M ÷ 10% = $20M; document labor 90,000 × 6 min × $45/h ≈ $0.4M; gate lower bounds, grain test and control counterfactual below.

Probing — challenge Q&A (master record) plus the statistical questions

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit?

Because I took the re-scope — 597 lanes first, not 1,000 — two levels up to the Global Head of Supply Chain with the spend curve and the lane owners' validation attached, then committed to delivering the remainder behind the same gate. And the gate held in public: one cohort failed twice on a timestamp format and I held it both times.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Completeness 60 → 95% is the record the whole program was for, and freight −10% ≈ $2M in six months is what the executive funded the next wave on. The 597 = 80% of spend live in month one is the proof the re-scope was right.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the $2M is the record as measured: minus ten percent on the onboarded lanes' spend in six months, kept as stated rather than doubled into a run-rate. My three-way split of it decomposes the record, never adds to it.

Probing — I'm concerned about your split of the $2M — tell me more.

The split is a judgment on how the ten points divide; to firm it I would tag every invoice line on the onboarded lanes by cause — off-guide carrier, mode change, expedite — for the six months and let the ledger set the shares.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — the gate is the product. Differently: the harness from cohort one, not cohort two.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Vendor and carrier onboarding at scale is a Pareto plus a gate — spend share first, a published data-quality bar a partner must clear before activation, and a floor (portal/templates) so the long tail is kept inside the standard rather than priced out of it.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The ask was 1,000 lanes in two months; the delivery was 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one at ≥95% valid ASN, completeness 60 → 95%, the remaining waves behind the same gate — and the executive accepted the phased plan in spend share, not lane count.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the spend Pareto was pre-validated by Transportation and Procurement and the scope proposal — 597 first, ~200 next, remainder after — could not be contradicted by any lane owner in the leadership meeting.

Who signed the scope?

The Global Head of Supply Chain accepted it after seeing the curve; the lane owners had already validated it. I priced the deferral in spend share, not lane count.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Carriers unable to produce valid ASNs, the scope being contradicted in the room, and bespoke maps per partner. Mitigations: XML/portal floor plus a hold rule, pre-validation, and a parameterized harness.

What was the entry bar for a cohort?

Standard and gate published to its carriers, mapping templates instantiated, test files in hand, a scorecard per lane. Then ≥95% valid ASN in test before activation.

How did you set the standard?

850/855/856/940/945/214/997 on X12, with XML or portal templates as the floor for carriers without EDI. The standard was the data, not the transport.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a lane?

Valid-ASN rate ≥95%, 997 acknowledgments flowing, tracking numbers joinable, completeness on its scorecard. Below that, the lane was fixed, not automated.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The cohort test day: every carrier's file through the harness in one sitting; defects classified as format, timestamp, missing segment or join key, each with an owner. That is where the timestamp defect surfaced.

Cutover — Which cohort did you hold and how did the business take it?

One cohort failed twice on a carrier timestamp format; I held it both times. Transportation accepted because the 95% gate was published before cohort one — a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion.

What did each wave deliver?

Wave 1: 597 lanes, 80% of spend, live in month one. Wave 2: ~200 lanes on a harness that made the work mostly configuration. Wave 3: the tail, including non-EDI carriers on templates.

Hypercare — How did you know you were done? What did you watch?

Completeness 60% → 95%, 100% visibility on high-value lanes, the harness reused without me, the method adopted as the sourcing SOP. Watched weekly: per-lane completeness, the 997 rate, tracking-number joinability. A lane that slipped below its scorecard went back to "fix," not "live."

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

Deferred ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend so the 597 could go live at the highest data quality in month one — priced, and delivered later behind the same gate. Escalated the re-scope to the Global Head of Supply Chain — two levels up — with the spend curve and the lane owners' validation attached. Refused: activating a cohort that failed the gate.

Did you deliver the remaining 400? · Do differently?

Yes — later waves, same gate; the first 597 carried the spend and the decision. Differently: the harness from cohort one, not cohort two.

Analytics — What does “completeness 95%” count — events or shipments?

The record is shipment-data completeness 60% → 95%; my illustrative arithmetic counts four milestones per shipment (480 of 800 received = 60%). At the event grain 95% means 5% of milestones are missing; at the shipment grain, with independent misses, only 0.95⁴ = 81.5% of shipments would be fully complete and the 60% start would be 0.6⁴ = 13.0%. Misses cluster by carrier, so the truth sits between the union bound (80% at the 95% start) and 95%. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH which grain the 60% and 95% were measured at.

Analytics — A ≥95% valid-ASN gate — what does it mean with a finite test sample?

The record is the gate: the cohort must clear ≥95% valid ASN in test. The cohort sizes are not in the record and I do not quote them. As arithmetic: 38 valid of 40 is 95% observed but its one-sided 95% lower bound is 85.1%; 190 of 200 gives 91.7%; 194 of 200 gives 94.2%. To demonstrate ≥95% rather than observe it you need 196 of 200 (98%) at n = 200 (lower bound 95.5%); at n = 40 it cannot be demonstrated even at 40 of 40 (92.8%). I would add that bound to the scorecard. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH test sizes and whether the bar was an observed or a bounded rate.

Analytics — How do you know the −10% freight spend came from visibility and not the market?

The record is −10% ≈ $2M on the onboarded lanes in six months; it carries no control. The deferred lanes are a free comparison group: about $5M of spend sits on the ~400 lanes that were not onboarded. If the deferred lanes’ spend moved −2% on the market alone, the visibility effect is (−10% − (−2%)) × $20M = $1.6M; at −5% it is $1.0M. I would pull that before I defend a causal claim. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH whether such a comparison was run.

I keep it as stated rather than doubling it into a run-rate." "How do you know the split?" — "I don't, to the dollar — the split is my model of where the ten percent came from, and the three slices sum to the record, never on top of it."

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured number is freight spend down ten percent, about two million dollars in six months on the five hundred ninety-seven lanes that carried eighty percent of spend, with completeness sixty to ninety-five percent and adoption eight x. Inside that two million I model roughly eight hundred thousand from catching off-guide carrier spend, six hundred thousand from mode discipline once the ETA was trusted, and six hundred thousand from expedites and detention that chase calls used to cause; those three are my split, the two million is the record. On top of it I model about four hundred thousand a year of document-handling labor that X12 replaced, and that piece is entirely mine."

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you pushed back on a senior leader’s request. Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit

Open (first two sentences): The Global Head of Supply Chain wanted 1,000 lanes of inbound visibility in two months. I told him the schedule would deliver 1,000 lanes of confident wrong data — and showed him the spend curve instead.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: scope, schedule and quality were all fixed, completeness was 60% and high-value lanes were blind. Task: own the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor. Action: I built the spend Pareto, pre-validated 597 / ~200 / remainder with Transportation and Procurement so no lane owner could contradict the cut, and took it up in spend share, not lane count. He accepted the phased plan off the curve; I committed completely. Result: 597 lanes carrying 80% of spend live in month one; ~400 lanes carrying ~20% deferred, priced, behind the same gate.

Land this number: 597 lanes = 80% of spend; ~400 deferred = ~20%. · Pivot to NACF: For NACF vendor onboarding I would lead with the volume Pareto and a gate, not a lane count. · Do NOT say: Do not name the executive’s reaction beyond “accepted the phased plan”; do not quote cohort sizes.

Q2 Tell me about a time you refused to compromise on quality under schedule pressure. Insist on the Highest Standards

Open (first two sentences): A cohort failed our ≥95% valid-ASN gate twice on a carrier timestamp format. I held it both times.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: month one, 597 lanes going live, the executive watching. Task: protect the standard without becoming the blocker. Action: the gate was published to carriers before cohort one — a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion; the failing cohort stayed in test, we fixed the format in the mapping template, retested, and activated off the gate. Result: completeness 60% → 95%; the gate is the product — automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers.

Land this number: Gate ≥95% valid ASN; held twice; completeness 60% → 95%. · Pivot to NACF: For NACF inbound the gate is “valid ASN rate before the vendor lane feeds placement.” · Do NOT say: Do not say how many lanes were in the failed cohort.

Q3 Tell me about a time you used data to decide where to focus. Dive Deep

Open (first two sentences): Lanes differed by orders of magnitude in spend, volume and criticality, so I scored all 1,000 before I scoped anything.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a flat 1,000-lane plan would spend most of the effort on the long tail. Task: decide what to do first. Action: spend × criticality × mode × volume scoring, plus a carrier EDI-capability survey, so every lane had a score and a feasibility. Result: 597 lanes = 80% of spend first, ~200 next, the rest behind the same gate — and a method written up as the sourcing SOP.

Land this number: S(597) = 80%; scoring on four factors. · Pivot to NACF: Placement analytics starts the same way: rank by volume × cost × criticality, then gate. · Do NOT say: Do not call the modeled Pareto fit the record — only the 597 = 80% point is record.

Q4 Tell me about a time you simplified a standard so more people could meet it. Invent and Simplify

Open (first two sentences): Carriers without EDI would have been priced out of the standard, so I made the standard the data, not the transport.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: some carriers could not produce valid ASNs over EDI. Task: keep the long tail inside one standard. Action: 850/855/856/940/945/214/997 on X12 with an XML or portal floor, parameterized per-partner mapping templates and a test harness instead of bespoke maps. Result: small carriers stayed in the standard; adoption 8×; the harness was reused for later waves.

Land this number: Adoption 8×; XML/portal floor. · Pivot to NACF: NACF vendors without EDI need the same floor: the standard is the data. · Do NOT say: Do not say I built the harness; engineers did to my standard.

  • Modeled wave 2 ≈ +10.7% of spend (~200 lanes).
  • Shipment-grain completeness at 95% events: 80%–95%.

  • Committed fully once accepted.

(iii) Failure — learning — what I would do differently
