Probing — challenge Q&A (master record) plus the statistical questions

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Think Big?

Because the move is structural, not incremental: the program stopped marketing a settings toggle and started selling an insurance contract — accountability transfer, priced per seller, self-selecting at marketplace scale. Thinking big here meant redesigning the incentive so thousands of independent sellers each make the right call on their own.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

The contract design leads because it is the thing I can honestly claim — the diagnosis, the structure, the gates, the measurement. Also important: the enforcement-posture recommendation (carrots until the alternative scales) and the kill condition — a protection promise the scorecard fails to honor stops enrollment, because one broken protection kills the program's legitimacy faster than slow adoption does.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

None claimed — the ≈$3.7M/yr is per ten points of enrolled GMV, modeled from the handling baseline, and the curve itself is the client's record. I give the unit economics so the investment argument is quantitative; I don't book the outcome.

Probing — I'm concerned you're taking credit for a program you didn't run to completion — tell me more.

That's exactly why the claim is scoped: mine is the diagnosis, the contract, the gates, wave one and the measurement pair; the client's is the curve. I'd rather under-claim a design than over-claim an outcome — the same discipline as S17, where I refuse the award dollar on the carrier bid.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Lesson: adoption stalls are usually incentive-design problems wearing feature-marketing costumes — price the trade and the funnel moves. Differently: start the refusal interviews before touching the funnel data; the sellers' own words located the drop-off faster than the analytics did, and I ran them second.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Amazon runs the reference version of this contract — observed handling, settings automation, protected labels, all-or-nothing accountability transfer. What I'd bring is the posture judgment: knowing when the contract's teeth fit the business (a mature fulfillment alternative, churn tolerance) and when they don't — and the measurement pair, because enrollments without retention is churn wearing a growth costume at any company.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The engagement's signed goals were a diagnosed stall, a rebuilt offer, a gated wave one and an honest measurement handoff — all four delivered. The goal was never "move the share N points under my name"; that outcome belongs to the owners who run the waves.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (12). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the stall had one explanation that survived the sellers' own words, the funnel data and the product team's review: a real benefit, unpriced and weakly communicated, traded against a visible loss of control. The exit memo named the funnel's biggest drop-off and the three refusal reasons in rank order.

Why interview churned sellers, not just refusers?

Because churn is the expensive refusal — someone who tried the program and left knows exactly which promise broke. Wave-one's protection-audit exit bar came directly from a churned seller's story about a scorecard ding the program should have excused.

Plan & Design — Why all-or-nothing instead of à la carte?

Because partial adoption keeps the adverse selection: a seller who keeps declared handling keeps the optimism that breaks promises. Cede all three controls and the marketplace can underwrite you; keep any one and you keep the risk. The bundle is what makes the contract insurable — and self-selecting.

Why does observed handling matter so much?

It kills self-reported optimism at the source. Declared handling is a bid for placement (declare one day, hit it 85%); observed handling is a measurement. Moving the promise onto what sellers actually do — rather than what they say — is the single largest accuracy lever in the seller-execution family.

What was the reference mechanism and what did you refuse to copy?

The dominant marketplace's equivalent: the same three-part bundle with full accountability transfer, enforced aggressively. We copied the contract structure and the grain. We did not copy the enforcement aggression or the churn tolerance — this client was still in assortment-growth mode, its fulfillment alternative subscale, so a churned seller was a strategic loss. Tiered protection instead of a cliff, revisited when the alternative scales.

Build & Test — What exactly did the shadow run verify?

Each enrolling seller's recent orders replayed under managed settings: projected promise accuracy, badge eligibility, and the scorecard-protection rules firing where they should. Day one verified on the seller's own history, not hoped — the same calibration-before-onboarding toll as my Flexport waves (S11).

How was the GMV-delta line computed and policed?

The client's analytics computed it per seller from the fast badge's measured conversion lift applied to that seller's assortment — their model, reproducible from logged lift data. My rule on it: the line is a projection and labeled as one in Seller Center; an unlabeled promise there would be the program lying to its own sellers.

Cutover — What were the wave-one graduation criteria?

Shadow results within tolerance of projection; observed-handling coverage on the cohort; inventory-feed accuracy at the seller's ship-from nodes (a stale feed causes cancellations — the worse defect); and the protection rules armed and audited. A wave that failed a criterion did not board the next cohort — publicly.

What was the kill condition?

A protection promise the scorecard fails to honor — an enrolled seller dinged for an excused carrier miss — stops enrollment until fixed. The contract's credibility is the product; one broken protection spends it faster than any growth banks it.

Hypercare — Why activations and 30-day retention?

Because enrollments without retention is churn wearing a growth costume. The pair is the honest read: throughput says the funnel works; retention says the contract kept its promise. A program graded on activations alone will board sellers it should have fixed first.

What flowed back into the offer after wave one?

Refusal and churn reason codes, as data: which component sellers feared (the label requirement led), which protection they doubted, where the GMV-delta line read as marketing instead of math. Overrides with reasons are data; silent refusals are a funnel you can't fix.

Cross-phase — What did you refuse?

An adoption target under my name (the curve is the client's), a hard label-purchase cliff (a tax on sellers with negotiated rates — tiered instead), enrollment pushes ahead of the shadow gate (day one must be verified), and counting activations without retention. Every refusal is in the contract document as a priced row.

Analytics — How sure are you that handling adherence is 85%?

The 85% is a record-from-docs baseline. As statistics: a seller with 100 shipments at 85% on time has a 95% Wilson interval of 76.7% to 90.7%; with 400 shipments, 81.2% to 88.2%. That is why “observed handling” needs enough scan history per seller before it replaces a declaration.

Analytics — What does the shadow gate actually test?

Replay a seller’s recent orders under managed settings and flip only within tolerance of the projection. At a projected 3% miss rate on 200 orders, flipping when misses ≤ 10 has a 4.0% false-reject rate on a seller who truly is at 3%, and detects a seller truly at 6% with 66% probability. Illustrative gate; the record is shadow-run-before-flip.

The math — step-by-step derivation

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The honest version first: what I delivered is the contract, not the curve. The program was stalled around forty percent of seller-fulfilled GMV because it asked sellers to trade control for an unpriced benefit — so we rebuilt it as insurance: give up transit settings, declared handling and the label, and the marketplace absorbs carrier-delay accountability and shows you what the fast badge is worth on your own assortment, in dollars. The unit economics are modeled and I label them: on the engagement record's own baseline — sellers declaring one-day handling and hitting it eighty-five percent of the time — every ten points of GMV that enrolls is worth about three point seven million dollars a year in avoided broken promises at the model's twelve-dollar breach cost. Wave one boarded behind published gates; the adoption curve after that belongs to the client's owners, and I say so."

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you found the real reason a program stalled. Dive Deep

Open (first two sentences): Adoption was stuck near forty percent and everyone wanted a better banner. The data said sellers were making a rational trade.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a managed-shipping program stalled at ~38–42% of seller-fulfilled GMV. Task: diagnose why. Action: enrollment-funnel and refusal interviews; the pattern was control traded for an unpriced, weakly communicated benefit, and declared one-day handling that sellers hit about 85% of the time — adverse selection. Result: the diagnosis moved the work from marketing to incentive design; the contract followed.

Land this number: 85% handling adherence; ~38–42% enrolled. · Pivot to NACF: NACF vendor ASN accuracy has the same incentive: declared dates are bids. · Do NOT say: Do not claim the adoption curve after wave one.

Q2 Tell me about a time you redesigned an offer instead of pushing the existing one. Invent and Simplify

Open (first two sentences): I made it one contract: give up transit settings, declared handling and take the label — and the marketplace absorbs carrier delays.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: sellers refused a toggle. Task: a design they would choose. Action: an all-or-nothing insurance bundle — transit automation + observed handling + participating label — with a personalized GMV-delta line in Seller Center showing the benefit in the seller’s own dollars. Result: signed contract design and wave-one mechanics; modeled ≈$3.7M a year per ten points of enrolled GMV.

Land this number: ≈$3.7M/yr per 10 pts (modeled). · Pivot to NACF: A priced, bundled commitment beats a toggle at NACF too. · Do NOT say: Do not call $3.7M a result.

Q3 Tell me about a time you were precise about what was yours. Earn Trust

Open (first two sentences): I claim the contract design and the wave-one mechanics. The adoption curve after that belongs to the client, and I say so.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a program whose headline number — enrolled GMV — was moved by many hands. Task: keep my claim to what I did. Action: the honesty pair — activations per week and 30-day retention — so growth cannot be faked by churn; and in every retelling, “modeled” is in the same breath as the dollar. Result: a claim that survives a reference check.

Land this number: Wave one boarded; $3.7M modeled. · Pivot to NACF: In an Amazon interview, the line between “I designed” and “we shipped” is the line. · Do NOT say: Do not annualize or extrapolate the curve.

Q4 Tell me about a time you held a high bar on rollout quality. Insist on the Highest Standards

Open (first two sentences): Day one is verified, not hoped: we replayed a seller’s orders under managed settings before flipping.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: flipping a seller wrongly breaks a promise. Task: a gate. Action: shadow-run before flip within tolerance of the projection; waves behind published graduation criteria — fail one, hold, publicly. As a design check: at n = 200 orders and a 3% projection the gate rejects a good seller 4.0% of the time and catches a 6% seller 66% of the time. Result: wave one boarded behind the criteria.

Land this number: c = 11 misses in 200 orders (illustrative). · Pivot to NACF: A NACF vendor-lane activation gate on valid-ASN rate, as in S9. · Do NOT say: Do not quote gate numbers as the record.

Trap drills NEW — the three nastiest follow-ups a statistically rigorous interviewer throws

T1 “Your $3.7M — which assumption dominates?”

The numbers: +10 pts: 100,000 × 12% × 50% × $12 × 52 = $3,744,000; miss reduction 6/15 pts → $1.87M/$4.68M; breach-through 25/75% → $1.87M/$5.62M; breach cost $6/$18 → $1.87M/$5.62M.

Say exactly: “It is a product of four inputs, so a halving or doubling of any one moves it proportionally — from about $1.9 million to $5.6 million on breach-through, $1.9 to $5.6 on the breach cost — and I call it modeled in the same breath; the client’s own data replaces each input.”

T2 “Eighty-five percent — what is the interval, and is 15% → 3% plausible?”

The numbers: n = 100: [76.7%, 90.7%]; n = 400: [81.2%, 88.2%]; 3% is illustrative.

Say exactly: “On a hundred shipments eighty-five percent is anywhere from about 77 to 91; the fifteen-to-three-point effect is an illustrative target — observed handling is the mechanism, and I would not quote three as measured.”

T3 “Enrollments are up — is the program working?”

The numbers: 200 enrollments × 50% retained = 100; 100 × 90% = 90; Wilson at 40/50 retained: [67%, 89%].

Say exactly: “Not necessarily — two hundred enrollments at fifty percent retention retain a hundred and a hundred enrollments at ninety retain ninety; raw enrollments say twice as good, retained activations say about the same, so the pair is activations per week and thirty-day retention, with a kill condition.”

E7 — Elementum design engagement (new-market broadband build) — equipment forecast-to-buy: a newsvendor first buy, risk-pooled central stock and a ±20% two-week re-forecast trigger, with the statistics behind each (design, not run) NEW

LPs this story serves here: Learn and Be Curious (primary — the toolkit applied, labeled) · Think Big (a repeatable launch template) · Are Right, A Lot (a decision under maximum uncertainty) · Customer Obsession (install delays caused by stockouts → 0)
Process family: Plan / Source seam — forecast-to-buy for a market launch (master E7; Google Fiber-style field-install build; finished-kit grain, no component explosion)
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each NEW
  • Learn and Be Curious — I applied the newsvendor critical ratio and risk pooling from the MIT MicroMasters toolkit to a cold-start launch — labeled as the toolkit, not as a program I ran.
  • Think Big — The design is a template for market N+1: build plan → adoption curve → requirement → measured lead times → first buy held central → forward-deploy as sell-through proves the curve.
  • Are Right, A Lot — Under maximum uncertainty I priced the decision — Cu ≈ $300 vs Co ≈ $3/month → CR 0.99 — and wrote the trigger that proves the curve wrong in two weeks, not two quarters.
  • Customer Obsession — The metric is install delays caused by stockouts → 0 in the first 90 days — a missed install is a customer waiting, priced at ≈$300 in the model.
Honesty label. Design engagement, not a run record. I have not owned a market-launch forecast end to end; the forecast number belongs to Planning — I own the equation, the measurement and the triggers (master E7). The newsvendor and pooling formulas are the MIT MicroMasters toolkit, labeled. All volumes, CVs and correlations here are illustrative. The master’s E-series does not name the client; the brief ties them to Google Fiber. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH that you are comfortable saying “Google Fiber” aloud — otherwise say “a broadband build-out client.” Finished-kit grain only — no component explosion.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. A broadband client launches a new market: build sites ramp by month, demand follows an adoption curve with no local history, and long-lead equipment takes weeks to months to arrive. A missed install means a re-roll and deferred revenue; extra stock means holding cost and stranding risk. Task. Design the forecast-to-buy chain so installs are never delayed by stockouts and stranded stock stays inside a band. Action. Four decisions, each with its arithmetic. First buy: a newsvendor — a missed install costs about $300, a unit held a month about $3, so the critical ratio is 0.990 and I order the 99th percentile of the first quarter’s demand for the core unit; accessories with a lower miss cost sit at the 70th. Held central: one pool covers several markets’ variance, √k instead of k — if the markets are uncorrelated, which I would measure. Trigger: actual take-rate versus the curve beyond ±20% for two consecutive weeks forces a new forecast version. Demand enters planning once, through the engine — no spreadsheets beside it. Result. Primary metric: install delays caused by stockouts → 0 in the first 90 days of a launch. Illustrative dollar: 2,000 installs a month × 3% reschedules × $300 ≈ $18K a month, about $216K a year per market, plus about $100K of stranding avoided — design arithmetic, not a program result.

20-second version NEW

For a new-market build I would buy the core unit at the 99th percentile of first-quarter demand because a missed install costs 100× a unit-month of holding, hold it central so uncertainty pools, and re-forecast when the take-rate leaves ±20% of the curve two weeks running.

NACF bridge — one sentence

NACF placement for a new FC or a new product launch has the same shape: a critical-ratio first buy, central-first, and a variance trigger — INV-C’s placement model plus INV-B’s service-level logic.

Situation / Task — the problem space

S — Market N launches: build sites ramp by month from the project system’s build plan; demand follows an adoption curve with no local history; kits and long-lead equipment have vendor lead times of weeks to months. The books: the project system for the build plan, Planning for the adoption curve, SAP MRP for supply.

T — Design the forecast-to-buy chain so installs are never delayed by stockouts and stranded stock stays inside a band — the equation, the measurement and the triggers; Planning owns the forecast number.

Baselines on the table (illustrative): 2,000 installs a month per market · 3% of installs rescheduled for stockouts · missed install ≈ $300 · holding ≈ $3/month · first buy ≈ $500K.

Action — five gated phases (design)
  • Discovery — restated the problem as a stock-and-flow map — build plan → sites passed → adoption curve × marketing → install demand → requirement by SKU × week → MRP on measured lead times → POs → central pool → market transfers. Exit bar: the three books of record named; the primary metric agreed.
  • Plan & Design — newsvendor first buy; central-first with risk pooling; re-forecast trigger ±20% for two weeks; invariants: demand enters planning once, lead times are measured distributions (the S8 rule), floors with hold-until dates so one market cannot steal another’s launch. Exit bar: each rule has its arithmetic on one page.
  • Build & Test — the arithmetic below — newsvendor cost curve, pooling under correlation, trigger false-alarm and detection — run on illustrative inputs; failure modes listed (a borrowed adoption curve, a brochure lead time, manual loads). Exit bar: every rule survives its own sensitivity.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (design) — launch plan: first buy held central; forward-deploy via transfers as sell-through proves the curve; bias sign published weekly from month two. Exit bar: install delays caused by stockouts = 0 in the first 90 days.
  • Hypercare / Close (design) — MAPE and bias per SKU × market × week; days-of-cover inside the band; the template re-run for market N+1. Exit bar: a floor breach is a governed screen with a reason code, not an email.

Influence and ownership. This is a design I owned the equation of; Planning owns the forecast, Finance the unit costs, Marketing the adoption curve. The backbone content is the invariant: demand enters planning exactly once, through the engine — manually loaded requirements beside it are blocked — and need floors stop one market starving another.

Results — design outcomes, all illustrative
  • Primary metric (design target) — install delays caused by stockouts → 0 in the first 90 days of a launch.
  • $ — 2,000 installs/month × 3% × $300 = $18,000/month ≈ $216,000/yr per market; 20% × $500K = $100,000 of stranding avoided by holding central (master, illustrative) — never a program result.
  • Statistics — critical ratio 0.990 → z = 2.33 → first-quarter buy 9,495 units on a 6,000-unit mean; pooling saves 50% of buffer at ρ = 0 and 31% at ρ = 0.3 (k = 4).
  • Quality — MAPE and bias per SKU × market × week from month two; bias sign before magnitude.
  • Honest limit — not run end to end; the forecast is Planning’s.

The five facets NEW — data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions; each labeled so the interviewer can see all five are present
1 · Data analysisData (design): build plan by month, adoption curve by archetype market, install-reschedule reasons joined to the supply view, vendor lead-time distributions (S8). Revealed (illustrative): the 3% baseline reschedule rate corresponds to ordering near the 79th percentile.
2 · Solution designOptions: forecast mean · p79 · p99 first buy; forward-deploy vs central-first; trigger at ±10% / ±20% / ±30%. Chosen: p99 on the core unit, p70 accessories, central-first, ±20% two weeks.
3 · Math & data logicMath: CR = 300 ÷ 303 = 0.9901; z = 2.330; Q = 6,000 + 2.330 × 1,500 = 9,495; expected short σ·L(z) = 5.0; pooling σ√(k + k(k−1)ρ); trigger DP on consecutive exceedances.
4 · ImplementationImplementation: stock-and-flow map → rules with arithmetic → invariants → launch plan → weekly MAPE/bias → template for N+1.
5 · Probe questionsProbe questions: why p99 · what if the CV is wrong · does pooling survive correlation · trigger false alarms — see Trap Drills.
Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to a statistician

Analytics — Show the newsvendor logic with numbers.

Critical ratio = Cu ÷ (Cu + Co) = 300 ÷ 303 = 0.9901, so order the 99.0th percentile of first-quarter demand: z = 2.330. With mean 6,000 and σ = 1,500 (CV 25%, illustrative) that is 9,495 units; expected shortfall σ·L(z) = 5.0 units, expected leftover 3,500; expected cost $12,009, versus $58,234 at the 79th percentile.

Analytics — Why does the master’s 3% reschedule rate matter?

Because it pins the baseline: a 3% shortfall on a 25% CV is L(z) = 0.03 ÷ 0.25 = 0.12 → z ≈ 0.8, the 79th percentile — a first buy near the 79th percentile reproduces the 3% and the $18K a month. The design moves it to the 99th percentile: expected shortfall 0.08% of installs. Illustrative CV; the master’s 3% is the anchor.

Analytics — Your CV is a guess. What if it is 35%?

Then a buy sized for 25% at the 99th percentile — 9,495 — sits at z = 1.66 on the true spread: a 4.8% chance of running short and an expected shortfall of 42 units, versus 5.0. The policy is robust in direction and fragile in tail: so the buy is re-sized as soon as the first weeks’ CV is observed, and the trigger exists for the same reason.

Analytics — Why is the central pool better — and when does it stop being better?

Pooling k markets cuts the buffer from k·zσ to z·σ·√(k + k(k−1)ρ): at k = 4 and independence, 50%; at ρ = 0.3, 31.1%; at ρ = 0.6, 16.3%; at ρ = 1, nothing. New markets share a product, a campaign and an adoption archetype, so ρ is probably not zero — I would measure it on analog markets before promising the saving. VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH

Shadow / closing — What would you do differently?

Measure the cross-market correlation on analog markets before quoting a pooling saving, and archetype-review the adoption curve with Marketing first — a curve borrowed from a non-analog market is the failure mode that voids everything else.

8Trigger, true take-rate +40% vs curvep = P(week beyond ±20%) = 1 − Φ((0.20 − 0.40) ÷ 0.10) = 97.72% per week (one side); P(2 in a row within 4 / 12 weeks) = 99.8% / 100.0%weekly noise 10% of curve (illustrative); consecutive-run recursion100% by week 4

Solution flow — the forecast-to-buy chain (proposed)

[Build plan] --milestones--> [Sites passed] --adoption curve x marketing--> [Install demand]
      --kit requirement at finished-kit grain--> [Need by SKU x week, as PIRs]
      --MRP (measured lead times, confirmed dates)--> [Planned orders -> POs]
      --ASN--> [Central 3PL pool] --STOs as sell-through proves the curve--> [Markets]

First buy: Q = mu + z*sigma, z = PhiInv(Cu/(Cu+Co)) ; central, pooled
Trigger: |take-rate / curve - 1| > 20% for 2 consecutive weeks -> new forecast version
Weekly: MAPE and bias per SKU x market x week; bias sign before magnitude

Total impact statement — say it like this

“This is a design, not a run record: I have not owned a launch forecast end to end, and the forecast number belongs to Planning. What I own is the equation, the measurement and the triggers. A missed install costs about three hundred dollars and a unit held a month about three, so the critical ratio is point nine nine and the first buy sits at the ninety-ninth percentile of the first quarter’s demand; held central it pools — half the buffer for four uncorrelated markets, about thirty percent if they share a correlation of point three, which I would measure. A take-rate outside plus or minus twenty percent for two weeks forces a new forecast. The dollars — about two hundred sixteen thousand a year per market and a hundred thousand of stranding avoided — are the master’s illustrative arithmetic, never a result.”

If challenged — the full answers

Did you run it? “No — a design engagement; Planning owns the forecast, I own the equation, the measurement and the triggers.” Is the CV real? “Illustrative; the buy is re-sized on the first weeks’ observed spread.” Is that Google Fiber? “The master’s design engagements are Elementum client work; I would name the client only if confirmed.” VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH

Behavioral pack NEW — four questions this story answers, four separate 90-second answers (each from a different moment of the story)

Q1 Tell me about a time you learned a new method to solve a problem. Learn and Be Curious

Open (first two sentences): I took the newsvendor model from my MIT MicroMasters coursework and applied it to a launch problem — and I label it as the toolkit.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a new-market build with no demand history. Task: design the first buy. Action: Cu ≈ $300 for a missed install, Co ≈ $3 a month for a unit held, critical ratio 0.990, so the 99th percentile of first-quarter demand for the core unit and the 70th for lower-cost accessories. I then stress-tested it: if the true CV is 35% rather than 25% the shortfall chance goes from about 1% to 4.8%, which is why the trigger exists. Result: a design with arithmetic, not a run record.

Land this number: CR 0.990; z 2.33; Q = 9,495 on a 6,000 mean. · Pivot to NACF: A new FC or product launch at NACF is the same critical-ratio problem. · Do NOT say: Do not say I ran the launch; do not present the CV as data.

Q2 Tell me about a time you designed for scale beyond the immediate problem. Think Big

Open (first two sentences): I designed the launch chain as a template for market N+1, not a one-off for market N.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: every market launch repeats the same chain — build plan, adoption curve, requirement, supply. Task: a design that a second and third market can reuse. Action: a stock-and-flow map, invariants — demand enters planning once, lead times are measured distributions, need floors with hold-until dates — and a weekly MAPE/bias loop. Result: a template; the forecast stays with Planning.

Land this number: One chain, three books of record, five invariants. · Pivot to NACF: A reusable placement template for each new NACF node. · Do NOT say: Do not claim a deployed template.

Q3 Tell me about a decision you made with very little data. Are Right, A Lot

Open (first two sentences): With no local history, the right question was not “what is demand” but “what does being wrong cost on each side.”

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: maximum uncertainty at launch. Task: first buy. Action: price both errors, buy to the critical ratio, hold it central so uncertainty pools, and write the trigger that proves the curve wrong fast: ±20% for two weeks. The trigger catches a true 25% shift within four weeks 77% of the time and false-fires about once every 20 series-years. Result: a decision with its own error rates.

Land this number: 77% detection by week 4 at +25%; 0.051 false triggers per series-year. · Pivot to NACF: Placement under uncertainty: price both errors, then set the trigger. · Do NOT say: Do not quote the weekly noise (10%) as measured.

Q4 Tell me about a time you put the end customer first in a supply decision. Customer Obsession

Open (first two sentences): The metric was install delays caused by stockouts — a customer waiting for a technician.

STAR (≈90 s): Situation: a missed install means a re-roll and deferred revenue. Task: make the stockout the thing the whole chain is measured on. Action: the primary metric joined reschedule reasons to the supply view; need floors so one market could not starve another’s launch; a governed screen with a reason code instead of an email. Result: design target zero delays caused by stockouts in the first 90 days.

Land this number: Target 0 delays in 90 days; ≈$300 per missed install (model). · Pivot to NACF: The NACF customer is the promise date; same joined-metric discipline. · Do NOT say: Do not claim installs were delivered.

Same story, three angles NEW — three different openers, headline metrics and LPs from the same facts
(i) Data / analytical

LP: Dive Deep; Are Right, A Lot

Open: “A 3% reschedule rate tells you the baseline was a 79th-percentile buy.”

Headline metric: CR 0.990; p99 buy 9,495; cost $12,009 vs $58,234

  • L(z) = 0.12 → z ≈ 0.8.
  • Expected cost curve is flat near the optimum, steep to the left.
  • CV 35% turns a 1% risk into 4.8%.
  • All illustrative.
(ii) Conflict / influence / backbone

LP: Have Backbone

Open: “Demand enters planning exactly once — manual loads beside the engine are blocked.”

Headline metric: one engine, one demand path; floors with hold-until dates

  • A floor breach is a governed screen with a reason code.
  • One market cannot starve another’s launch.
  • A borrowed adoption curve is a stop-the-line item.
  • Design stance, not a run record.
(iii) Failure — learning — what I would do differently

LP: Learn and Be Curious

Open: “I have not owned a launch forecast end to end.”

Headline metric: pooling saves 31% at ρ = 0.3, not 50%

  • Measure ρ on analog markets before quoting a saving.
  • The forecast belongs to Planning.
  • The toolkit is labeled.
  • VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH client naming.
Trap drills NEW — the three nastiest follow-ups a statistically rigorous interviewer throws

T1 “Why the 99th percentile for the first buy — is that not wasteful?”

The numbers: CR = 300 ÷ 303 = 0.9901; z = 2.330; p99 buy 9,495: cost $12,009; p79 buy 7,200: $58,234; leftover 3,500 units × $3 = $10,500.

Say exactly: “Because a missed install costs a hundred unit-months of holding: at the ninety-ninth percentile the expected first-quarter cost is about $12 thousand against $58 thousand at the seventy-ninth, and the leftover — about thirty-five hundred units — is not waste, it is next quarter’s demand.”

T2 “Does the pooling benefit survive correlated markets?”

The numbers: k = 4: ρ = 0 → saves 50%; ρ = 0.3 → 31.1%; ρ = 0.6 → 16.3%; ρ = 1 → 0%.

Say exactly: “Only partly — half the buffer at zero correlation, about thirty-one percent at a correlation of point three, sixteen at point six; new markets share a product and a campaign, so I would measure it on analog markets before promising any number.”

T3 “Your trigger is ±20% for two weeks. What are its false alarms and its power?”

The numbers: Weekly noise 10% (illustrative): no shift → 0.051 false triggers per series-year (both sides); true +25% → 77% within 4 weeks; +40% → 100%; +10% → 22% within 12 weeks.

Say exactly: “With ten percent weekly noise the trigger false-fires about once in 20 series-years, catches a true twenty-five percent shift within a month about 77 times in a hundred, and is nearly blind to a ten percent bias — which is why the bias sign publishes weekly beside it.”

Backup bench — use only if a primary doesn't fit

Three fallbacks for an analytics manager: two master-packet backups plus one doc-derived Flexport micro-story — S4 (Sephora — the in-transit promise rule I got wrong; the failure / Are Right, A Lot question, promise math at lane σ) and S7 (Elementum — a contract manufacturer's planning data made safe to plan on, finished-goods grain only; Learn & Be Curious by math, not meetings); the doc-derived third is the FFA Asia latency investigation from the Flexport program record (Dive Deep on production evidence; record-from-docs labels, facts identical to the same bench in Josyula's packet). S9 (the 1,000 → 597 EDI story) was promoted to a full primary above. Tell the ten primaries first.

Bench discipline. The ten primaries above are the stories to tell first. Reach for a bench story only when the question genuinely does not fit a primary, or when the interviewer asks for "another example" on an LP already spent. Each entry is a 60-second pitch, a compressed STAR, one results line and two probes — enough to answer and stop; no full probing bank or derivation. Where a bench story is another interviewer's primary, the red note says so: use it only if the question demands it, and keep the facts identical to that packet.

Backup — only if askedS4 — Sephora S/4HANA — the in-transit promise rule I got wrong, fixed through the signed policy, not a side configuration

LPs this backup can serve: Are Right, A Lot (the failure question) · Customer Obsession (backup) · Earn Trust (owning the miss) · Learn and Be Curious — the designated policy-failure story
Basis. The v1 rule was mine; the indictment came from my own program's reports. Lane σ ≈ 0.3 day and the ~0.49-day window are the record's promise math; the record carries no lane counts and no dollar for the conversion lost while I was wrong — I will not invent the loss figure.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. My first ISM ruleset barred every channel from promising units in transit; the Aging In-Transit report showed dotcom starved of fast movers one day from the building. Task. Correct my own policy without weakening oversell protection, through the same three signatories. Action. Discovery: conversion-loss evidence from the aging and replenishment reports — the entry bar to reopen the policy; I stated that the v1 rule was mine. Plan & Design: promise math — lane σ ≈ 0.3 day, a ~0.49-day window ≈ 95% coverage; Promisable = in-transit × 1[ETA ≤ window] × 1[on-time ≥ floor]; Aging In-Transit as the kill switch; both blanket options rejected in writing. Build & Test: back-test against receiving history; four regression cells added to the node × channel matrix. Cutover: released on the normal monthly cadence as a signed FSD change, lane by lane — no hotfix, no side configuration. Hypercare: kill switch armed; never fired. Lesson: both sides of a rule get a number before it ships. Result. Primary metric: sellable units on qualifying in-transit lanes — starved → restored inside a per-lane promise window, oversell increase = zero, kill switch never fired. ≈$0.5M/yr modeled margin recovered — the record has no dollar for the conversion lost while I was wrong, and I will not invent one.

Compressed STAR
  • S — v1 of my own ISM ruleset: no channel could promise in-transit units — safe-feeling, and starving dotcom of fast movers reliably one day from the building.
  • T — Fix my own signed policy without weakening oversell protection; take the miss back through the same three signatories.
  • A — Evidence pack from Aging In-Transit and Replenishment; reframed as promise math (σ ≈ 0.3 d → ~0.49-d window ≈ 95%); rule with on-time floor and kill switch; back-test against receiving history; four regression cells; released lane by lane on the monthly cadence as an FSD change.
  • R — Starved lanes restored inside the window; oversell increase = 0; kill switch never fired; re-signed by the same three signatories; lesson institutionalized — the Locus 98% gate (S2) and the 0.5% tolerance (S18) were set from data before the event.
Results line

Sellable units on qualifying in-transit lanes starved → restored inside a back-tested ~0.49-day window (record) · oversell increase = 0; kill switch armed, never fired (record) · four regression cells added (record) · released as a signed FSD change on the normal cadence, no hotfix (record) · ≈$0.5M/yr modeled margin recovered (inputs illustrative).

The math — step-by-step derivation

Table 1 — before: the problem in numbers.

StepQuantityFormula → substitutionInputs (basis)Value
1The v1 rulePromisable = 0 for ALL in-transit inventoryequivalent to claiming σ → ∞ or on-time rate → 0 — infinite variability (record)everything in transit hidden
2One concrete lanePromisable = 120 × 0 = 0lane with InTransit = 120 units, ETA 0.4 day away (illustrative)120 sellable units hidden
3Across qualifying lanes≈300 fast-mover units/day × 0 promisableillustrative volume≈300 units/day sat unsellable one day from the building
4What starving costs (one SKU)100 × 60% × $20 = $1,200/day100 units one day out; 60% would convert; $20 margin (illustrative, not program data)$1,200/day under-sell
5The ignored other side100 × (1 − 0.97) = 3 late units; 3 × $15 = $45/dayon-time rate 97%; $15 expedite (illustrative)$45/day over-sell exposure — vs $1,200: the “safe” rule priced one side only

Table 2 — the fix: step by step, values substituted.

StepQuantityFormula → substitutionInputs (basis)Value
1Measure lane variabilityσ = 0.3 daytrailing receiving history (record)σ = 0.3 day
2Choose coveragez at 95% = 1.645normal approximation on lane transit (toolkit label)z = 1.645
3Promise windoww = z × σ = 1.645 × 0.3 = 0.4935rows 1–2w ≈ 0.49 day
4Coverage checkΦ(w / σ) = Φ(0.49 / 0.3) = Φ(1.63) ≈ 0.95share of arrivals inside the window≈95% covered
5Corrected rulePromisable = InTransit × 1[ETA ≤ w] × 1[OTR ≥ floor]OTR = on-time rate over trailing receipts; floor = per-lane admission threshold, 95% here (floor illustrative)lane by lane, not all-or-nothing
6Lane that qualifiesETA 0.4 ≤ 0.49 → 1; OTR 97% ≥ 95% → 1; Promisable = 120 × 1 × 1 = 120the Table 1 lane, re-evaluated (ETA and OTR illustrative)its 120 units become promisable

Why not just hotfix it — you knew it was wrong?

Because the error was in the policy, so the fix had to be too — in the governed artifact, with regression, through the same signatures. A side configuration would have fixed the symptom and left the policy lying about what it allowed.

What did the miss cost?

Backup — only if askedS7 — Elementum — making a contract manufacturer's planning data safe to plan on: six sites, three waves, five months, zero auto-loaded revision overlaps

LPs this backup can serve: Learn and Be Curious (primary — learned the CM's data by math, not meetings) · Dive Deep · Earn Trust (external partner)
Basis. I owned the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan and the weekly Flextronics call; engineers built the normalization layer and quarantine to the spec; the commercial relationship belonged to the customer's procurement — my leverage was evidence. The $1.5M / 580K belong to the p99 program this backbone enabled (S8). Finished-goods grain only here; no tolerance value — the record does not carry one.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Flextronics supplied the planning data our component-demand visibility depended on, and it carried two traps documentation never confesses: pass-through assemblies that double-time demand if loaded as stocked, and overlapping revisions that rewrite history if you auto-load one. Six site systems carried six definitions of yield, lead time and order status. Task. I owned the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan and the weekly Flextronics call — with no commercial leverage; evidence was my only lever. Action. Discovery: sampled finished goods and reconciled the CM's planning data against its own MRP — the trap signature found by math, named in a discovery memo. Plan & Design: an explicit marker for pass-through items; an effective-date quarantine with human disposition — never auto-load; one supply equation (on-hand + production plan + released planned orders) signed by customer planning. Build & Test: mapping tables owned by named site super-users; a per-site reconciliation report as the go-live gate. Cutover: three waves — two cleanest high-volume sites, then three on parameterized maps, then the hostile legacy ERP by snapshot-diffing at six weeks against a three-week average, priced up front. Hypercare: the weekly call left standing — it had become theirs. Lesson: reconcile to the source's math, not its documentation. Result. Primary metric: contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade data — 0 → 6 in three waves and five months, zero auto-loaded revision overlaps. ≈$0.3M/yr modeled — the six sites and zero overlaps are measured; the dollars are my model; the $1.5M belongs to S8.

Compressed STAR
  • S — A contract manufacturer's planning feed with two silent traps; six sites, six definitions; one hostile legacy ERP (flat files, no change-data-capture); no commercial leverage.
  • T — Own ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the 2/3/1 wave plan, and chair the weekly Flextronics call.
  • A — Found the traps by sampling finished goods against the CM's own MRP; designed the two mechanisms that made the traps impossible rather than policed (explicit marker; effective-date quarantine, human disposition); reconciliation report as the per-site gate, not a date on a slide; wave 3's six weeks priced to leadership as the plan, not a slip; made the weekly call the CM's own protection against blame for misses its feed didn't cause.
  • R — Planning-grade sites 0 → 6 in 3 waves / 5 months; zero auto-loaded overlaps ever; phantom double-timing eliminated at source; backbone handed to the 200-lane program (S8).
Results line

CM sites with planning-grade data 0 → 6 in three waves / five months (record) · auto-loaded revision overlaps = 0, ever (record) · hardest site 6 weeks vs the 3-week average, priced up front and absorbed in plan (record) · ≈$0.3M/yr (modeled — reconciliation labor, expedites fired at false shortages, overlap rework); the record has no dollar for this story; the $1.5M belongs to S8.

Two probes

How did you get an external supplier with no obligation to care?

By making the weekly call theirs: it protected Flextronics from blame for misses their feed didn't cause. Procurement held the commercial relationship; my lever was the evidence in the discovery memo, confirmed by their own engineering.

Why three waves instead of one?

So one surprise could not spend six sites of credibility — the two cleanest high-volume sites first, three on parameterized maps second, the hostile ERP last with its six weeks priced up front. Speed was traded, never quality: a site went live off its matched reconciliation report, not a date.

Backup — only if askedDoc bench — Flexport FFA — the Asia latency investigation: a 21-second P95 traced to routing, decomposed to the layer, fixed in sequence (Dive Deep)

LPs this backup can serve: Dive Deep (primary — decomposition to the layer) · Deliver Results · Learn & Be Curious · Insist on the Highest Standards (a smoke-test protocol per change)
Basis. Doc-derived micro-story from the program record (the FFA Asia latency investigation, post-MVP 2022) — every figure is record-from-docs: the investigation document, written when the FF App MVP had launched to 11 partners in 10 countries and 48 of 53 import partners (90.5%) had migrated. Program-lead voice: I owned the investigation, the decomposition and the fix sequence; infrastructure teams executed the WAF and CDN changes. Not in the master packet's S-numbering; supplements canon, never overrides it. The same bench appears in Josyula's packet — facts identical.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Weeks after the FF App MVP launched to 11 partners in 10 countries — with 48 of 53 import partners already migrated — the monthly business review showed Asia page loads at a P95 of 21.19 seconds against 3.3 in the US. Task. Find the layer, not the anecdote, and fix it without a re-architecture in the middle of a live partner-facing launch. Action. I decomposed the problem before prescribing: static assets ran ~5s P95 in China against ~500ms in the US, and the app's domain had no geo-based DNS or reverse proxy — Asia traffic was riding the public internet across the Pacific; a double CDN sat on the static path. The fix was a sequence, not a project: enable the WAF, remove the double CDN (direct to S3), then a regional CDN proposal with a smoke-test protocol per change, then China tuning, then Asia tuning — each with a date and an owner. Result. Root cause isolated to routing — not the app, not the partners; two fixes shipped inside the investigation window and the regional plan scheduled with dates; and a standing rule imported into every later launch: the pilot's geography is a performance requirement, not a rollout detail. record-from-docs

Compressed STAR
  • S — Post-MVP, the MBR surfaced Asia P95 page load at 21.19s vs 3.3s in the US (17-day measurement window); 48 of 53 import partners (90.5%) already migrated — the app had to hold where the partners were.
  • T — Isolate the layer causing Asia latency and sequence the fix set without destabilizing a live partner-facing launch.
  • A — Split static from dynamic: static JS ~5s P95 in China vs ~500ms US; found no geo-based DNS / reverse proxy on the domain (public-internet routing) and a double CDN on static assets; sequenced WAF enablement → double-CDN removal (direct to S3) → regional CDN proposal → China tuning → Asia tuning, each gated by a smoke-test protocol with a named date and owner.
  • R — Root cause isolated to routing; WAF and double-CDN fixes shipped inside the window; CDN / China / Asia work scheduled with owners; the investigation became the template for launch-geography checks.
Results line

P95 page load 21.19s Asia vs 3.3s US (record-from-docs) · static ~5s China vs ~500ms US (record-from-docs) · 48 of 53 partners migrated = 90.5% (record-from-docs; canon's "48 of 53 within a month" — consistent) · two fixes shipped, three sequenced with dates (record-from-docs) · no post-fix latency figure exists in the record — never invent one.

Two probes

How did you know it was routing and not the application?

By decomposition before remediation: the static/dynamic split put ~5s of the China time on assets the app server never touched, and the same assets served in ~500ms from the US — the code was constant, the path was not. The domain's missing geo-DNS / reverse proxy and the double CDN explained both halves of the gap without a single application change.

Why not just stand up a China region?

A region is a re-architecture with a compliance tail; the evidence said the bottleneck was routing and CDN topology, which fixes in weeks. The sequenced plan — WAF, double-CDN removal, regional CDN, tuning — bought most of the win at a fraction of the risk, and the smoke-test protocol meant each step was reversible.

Canon numbers (basis labels): 21.19s vs 3.3s P95 (record-from-docs) · ~5s vs ~500ms static (record-from-docs) · 48/53 = 90.5% migrated (record-from-docs) · 11 partners / 10 countries MVP (record — matches S12 canon) · fix sequence WAF → double-CDN → regional CDN → China → Asia with smoke tests (record-from-docs). Never: a post-fix latency number (none in the record); never present scheduled items as delivered.
